and note that the definition of
\mathcal{L}
involves taking a minimum over all possible permutations of estimated labels.

The proof of the following result and its corollary are contained in Appendices
E.4
and
E.5
, respectively:

Proposition 4
.

Suppose
\frac{n}{k}\geq 6
and
\theta
is balanced. Let
\mathcal{M}:\{0,1\}^{n\times n}\to\mathbf{M}_{n\times k}
be a symmetric
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-node DP mechanism. For any adjacency matrix
A\in\{0,1\}^{n\times n}
and any
\xi\in(0,1/3)
, we have

\mathbb{P}(\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{M}(A),\theta)\leq\xi)\leq\frac{1}{(1-\xi)(1+(k-1)e^{-2\varepsilon})}+\frac{2(k-1)\delta}{1-\xi}.

Corollary 3
.

Suppose
\frac{n}{k}\geq 6
,
\theta
is balanced, and
k\asymp 1
. Let
\mathcal{M}:\{0,1\}^{n\times n}\to\mathbf{M}_{n\times k}
be a symmetric
(\epsilon,\delta)
-node DP mechanism. Suppose
A\sim SBM(n,k,B,\theta)
. If
\mathbb{P}(\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{M}(A),\theta)\leq\xi)\geq 1-\eta
, with
\xi\in(0,1/3)
, then

\epsilon\geq\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{1-\eta-\xi-2k\delta}{\eta+\xi+2k\delta}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\log(k-1).

In particular, if
\eta,\xi,\delta=O\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}\right)
, we have
\epsilon\succsim\log(n)
. Moreover, if
\eta=O\left(\frac{1}{\log(n)}\right)
and
\xi,\delta=O\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}\right)
, we have
\epsilon\succsim\log(\log(n))
.

Remark 16
.

The lower bound on
\epsilon
in Corollary
3
shows that letting
k\rightarrow\infty
or allowing the error probabilities
\eta
or
\delta
to be
\Theta(1)
would not preclude a scenario where
\xi=o(1)
(i.e., consistency) but
\epsilon
stays constant. Furthermore, if
\eta=O\left(\frac{1}{\log(n)}\right)
and
\xi\asymp\frac{1}{\log(n)}
, as in the utility guarantees for some of our algorithms above, the lower bound produced by Corollary
3
becomes
\epsilon\succsim\log(\log(n))
.

Our results point to a general requirement of
\epsilon\succsim\log(n)
, under certain assumptions on
(\xi,\eta,\delta)
. This is also the lower bound achieved by Klopp and Zadik
[
48
]
, who further established matching upper bounds with a privacy mechanism based on the exponential mechanism. However, we emphasize that the methods studied in our paper are all
polynomial-time
. In fact, if one were to combine an exponential-time alternative in
[
15
]
with our degree truncation techniques, one could derive a consistent community estimation technique requiring only
\epsilon\asymp\log(n)
in the
k=2
case.

Notably, both of our lower bounds and indeed, all existing lower bounds in the literature, do not involve any explicit dependence on the expected degree
d
. Since we have different scaling requirements on
\epsilon
for different algorithms, generally depending on whether
d\asymp\log(n)
or
d=\widetilde{\Theta}(\sqrt{n})
, an important open question is whether one could derive lower bounds on
\epsilon
which also depend on
d
.

7
Discussion

We have presented several new methods for community estimation in stochastic block models that preserve node-level differential privacy. A central idea is that under standard assumptions on the underlying SBM parameters, the observed graph will have maximum degree of order the average maximum degree
d
, which is somewhat smaller than the number of nodes in the graph. Although a private algorithm must be private even for worst-case inputs, creating substantial technical difficulties, utility analysis is conducted w.h.p., in which case it suffices to consider graphs which lie in the bounded-degree set.

Our first approach leverages a Lipschitz extension idea combined with a method for differentially private PCA previously proposed in the literature. Graphs with bounded degree have adjacency matrices with low sensitivity with respect to the PCA score function. We show how to extend the score function to graphs of arbitrary degree, preserving the sensitivity bound while maintaining computational feasibility. Our second approach is based on constructing a smooth projection from the space of all graphs to the space of bounded-degree graphs, with a calculable high-probability bound on the local sensitivity of the projection. This can be combined with an edge-private SBM estimation procedure; we present several options and the resulting conditions on the privacy parameters required for consistent community recovery.

While we believe that our paper makes substantial progress toward the problem of node-private SBM recovery, several open questions remain. Given the gap between the assumptions we have imposed on
\epsilon
to ensure consistent estimation for all of our algorithms and the lower bounds on
\epsilon
we have derived, an important question is to understand which, if any, of our algorithms give the best possible rates in terms of
\epsilon
. We note that
[
48
]
derive matching upper and lower bounds, but the algorithm they analyze in their upper bound is not computationally feasible. Thus, the open question is whether one can establish larger lower bounds on
(\epsilon,\delta)
if one is restricted to
polynomial-time
algorithms. Another related question is whether one can establish lower bounds for private estimation in terms of a quantity which depends more explicitly on the expected degree
d
, which might then match our upper bounds in certain settings.

Our Lipschitz extension method based on private PCA proceeds by extracting one eigenvector at a time and using eigenvector deflation. This is due to two bottlenecks: One is that the theoretical analysis of private PCA seems to rely heavily on the fact that we are only extracting
q=1
eigenvector. Furthermore, existing polynomial-time methods for exactly sampling from a Bingham distribution work only when
q=1
. If it were possible to overcome both of these issues, one could plausibly obtain a private exponential mechanism-based approach with fewer conditions on eigengaps between successive eigenvalues of the population-level matrix
P
.

Another important question concerns a well-studied topic in the analysis of SBMs, namely differences between consistency (as we have studied in our paper) and
exact recovery
, where one wishes to correctly classify every single node, w.h.p. Various papers in the SBM literature, e.g.,
[
29
]
, demonstrate how an initial consistent community assignment can be further refined to an exact assignment, in parameter regimes where exact recovery is possible. It is unclear whether the second refinement step could be privatized; if so, it could be combined with our methods to obtain a nice approach for private exact recovery in SBMs.

Finally, one might be interested in analyzing community estimation in SBMs under alternative notions of privacy. One that naturally comes to mind is where
G^{\prime}
is defined as adjacent to
G
if
G^{\prime}
is obtained from
G
by
removing
a single node entirely from the graph. Analyzing such a notion of privacy seems rather beyond the scope of this paper, and would likely give rise to other methodological and theoretical innovations.

Acknowledgments

The authors thank the Summer Research in Mathematics (SRIM) program at Cambridge for providing a stimulating environment for seeding this research in the summer of 2024. The research was partly supported by funds from the Leverhulme Trust.

Appendix A
Auxiliary Results

A.1
Linear algebraic results

Lemma 4

(Eckart-Young-Mirsky Theorem
[
7
]
)
.

Suppose
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
, and let
A=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sigma_{i}u_{i}v_{i}^{T}
denote the SVD. For
1\leq k\leq r
, let
A_{k}:=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sigma_{i}u_{i}v_{i}^{T}
. Then

A_{k}\in\arg\min_{B\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}:\text{rank}(B)\leq k}\|A-B\|,

where
\|\cdot\|
denotes the spectral or Frobenius norm.

We now state a few results concerning singular values of sums of matrices.

Lemma 5

(Lemma 2.1 in
[
64
]
)
.

Let
A,B\in\mathbb{R}^{f\times\ell}
. Then for
1\leq i
and
j\leq\min\{f,\ell\}
, we have

\displaystyle\sigma_{i+j-1}(A+B)\leq\sigma_{i}(A)+\sigma_{j}(B).

The next few lemmas concern matrix perturbation bounds.

Lemma 6

(Weyl’s inequality, Lemma 2.2 in
[
51
]
)
.

Let
A,A^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be symmetric matrices. Let
\sigma_{j}
and
\sigma_{j}^{*}
denote the
j^{\text{th}}
eigenvalues of
A
and
A^{*}
, respectively. Then

|\sigma_{j}-\sigma_{j}^{*}|\leq\|A-A^{*}\|_{2}.

Now we have a result concerning the operator norm of the Hadamard product. We provide a short proof for completeness.

Lemma 7

(
[
40
]
)
.

Let
A,B\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, with
A^{T}=A
and
A\succeq 0
or
A\preceq 0
. Then

\displaystyle||A\odot B||_{2}\leq\mathop{\max}\limits_{i,j}|A_{ij}|\cdot||B||_{2}.

Proof.

First suppose
A\succeq 0
and
A=A^{T}
. Let
r=\text{rank}(A)
. Then there exist vectors

u_{1},\dots,u_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
such that
A_{ij}=u_{i}^{T}u_{j}
. Define a linear map
V:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{rn}
by

\displaystyle Ve_{i}=e_{i}\otimes u_{i},

where
e_{i}
is the
i^{\text{th}}
standard basis vector. Then
A\odot B=V^{T}(B\otimes I_{r})V
, so

\|A\odot B\|_{2}\leq\|V^{T}\|_{2}\,\|B\otimes I_{r}\|_{2}\,\|V\|_{2}=||V||_{2}^{2}||B||_{2}.

We have

\displaystyle V^{T}V=\mathrm{diag}(\|u_{1}\|_{2}^{2},\dots,\|u_{n}\|_{2}^{2})=\mathrm{diag}(A_{11},\dots,A_{nn}),

so
||V||_{2}^{2}=\mathop{\max}\limits_{i}A_{ii}\leq\mathop{\max}\limits_{i,j}|A_{ij}|
. This implies
||A\odot B||_{2}\leq\mathop{\max}\limits_{i,j}|A_{ij}|\cdot||B||_{2}
. The case when
A\preceq 0
works in the same way, by considering
-A
. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 8

(Davis-Kahan Theorem, Lemmas 2.3 and 2.4 in
[
64
]
)
.

Consider the SVD

\displaystyle X=\begin{bmatrix}U&U_{\perp}\end{bmatrix}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\Sigma_{1}&0\\
0&\Sigma_{2}\end{bmatrix}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}V^{T}\\
V_{\perp}^{T}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{f\times\ell},

where
U\in\mathbb{R}^{f\times k}
and
U_{\perp}\in\mathbb{R}^{f\times(f-k)}
are orthonormal,
\Sigma_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
and
\Sigma_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{(f-k)\times(\ell-k)}
are diagonal, and
V\in\mathbb{R}^{f\times k}
and
V_{\perp}\in\mathbb{R}^{f\times(f-k)}
are orthonormal. Let
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{f\times\ell}
be a perturbation matrix and let
\hat{X}=X+Y
, where
\hat{X}
has SVD

\displaystyle\hat{X}=\begin{bmatrix}\hat{U}&\hat{U}_{\perp}\end{bmatrix}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\hat{\Sigma_{1}}&0\\
0&\hat{\Sigma_{2}}\end{bmatrix}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}\hat{V}^{T}\\
\hat{V}_{\perp}^{T}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{f\times\ell}.

In the above,
\hat{U},\hat{U}_{\perp},\hat{\Sigma_{1}},\hat{\Sigma_{2}},\hat{V},\hat{V}_{\perp}
, have the same structure as
U,U_{\perp},\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2},V,V_{\perp}
, respectively. Let
z_{21}=U_{\perp}U_{\perp}^{T}YVV^{T}
,
z_{21}=UU^{T}YV_{\perp}V_{\perp}^{T}
,
a_{1}=\sigma_{min}(U^{T}\hat{X}V)
, and
b_{1}=||U_{\perp}^{T}\hat{X}V_{\perp}||_{2}
. Suppose
\sigma_{1}\geq\dots\geq\sigma_{k}
are the singular values of
U^{T}\hat{U}
. Let
\Theta(U,\hat{U})\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
be a diagonal matrix such that
\Theta_{ii}(U,\hat{U})=\cos^{-1}(\sigma_{i})
, for all
i
. If
a_{1}^{2}>b_{1}^{2}+\min\{z_{12}^{2},z_{21}^{2}\}
, then

\displaystyle||\sin(\Theta)(U,\hat{U})||_{2}\leq\frac{a_{1}||z_{21}||_{2}+b_{1}||z_{12}||_{2}}{a_{1}^{2}-b_{1}^{2}-\min\{z_{12}^{2},z_{21}^{2}\}}.

Furthermore, we have the inequality

\displaystyle||\sin(\Theta)(U,\hat{U})||_{2}\leq\left\|\hat{U}\hat{U}^{T}-UU^{T}\right\|_{2}\leq 2||\sin(\Theta)(U,\hat{U})||_{2}.

We now state the Davis-Kahan Theorem for square matrices:

Lemma 9

(Theorem 2 in
[
72
]
)
.

Suppose
A,\hat{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
are symmetric, with eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}\geq\dots\geq\lambda_{p}
and
\hat{\lambda}_{1}\geq\dots\geq\hat{\lambda}_{p}
, respectively. Fix
1\leq r\leq s\leq p
, and assume
\min\{\lambda_{r-1}-\lambda_{r},\lambda_{s}-\lambda_{s+1}\}>0
, where we define
\lambda_{0}=\infty
and
\lambda_{p+1}=-\infty
. Let
d=s-r+1
, and let
V=[v_{r},v_{r+1},\dots,v_{s}]\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times d}
and
\hat{V}=[\hat{v}_{r},\hat{v}_{r+1},\dots,\hat{v}_{s}]\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times d}
have orthonormal columns satisfying
Av_{j}=\lambda_{j}v_{j}
and
\hat{A}\hat{v}_{j}=\hat{\lambda}_{j}\hat{v}_{j}
, for
j=r,r+1,\dots,s
. Then

\displaystyle||\sin(\Theta)(V,\hat{V})||_{F}\leq\frac{2\min\{\sqrt{d}||A-\hat{A}||_{2},||A-\hat{A}||_{F}\}}{\min\{\lambda_{r-1}-\lambda_{r},\lambda_{s}-\lambda_{s+1}\}}.

Moreover, there exists an orthogonal matrix
\hat{Q}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
such that

\displaystyle||\hat{V}\hat{Q}-V||_{F}\leq\frac{2^{3/2}\min\{\sqrt{d}||A-\hat{A}||_{2},||A-\hat{A}||_{F}\}}{\min\{\lambda_{r-1}-\lambda_{r},\lambda_{s}-\lambda_{s+1}\}}.

We now state a generalization of the Davis-Kahan Theorem for non-square matrices:

Lemma 10

(Theorem 3 in
[
72
]
)
.

Suppose
A,\hat{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}
have singular values
\sigma_{1}\geq\dots\geq\sigma_{\min\{p,q\}}
and
\hat{\sigma}_{1}\geq\dots\geq\hat{\sigma}_{\min\{p,q\}}
, respectively. Fix
1\leq r\leq s\leq\mathrm{rank}(A)
and assume
\min\{\sigma_{r-1}^{2}-\sigma_{r}^{2},\sigma_{s}^{2}-\sigma_{s+1}^{2}\}>0
, where we define
\sigma_{0}^{2}=\infty
and
\sigma_{\mathrm{rank}(A)+1}^{2}=-\infty
. Let
d=s-r+1
, and let
V=[v_{r},v_{r+1},\dots,v_{s}]\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times d}
and
\hat{V}=[\hat{v}_{r},\hat{v}_{r+1},\dots,\hat{v}_{s}]\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times d}
have orthonormal columns satisfying
Av_{j}=\sigma_{j}u_{j}
and
\hat{A}\hat{v}_{j}=\hat{\sigma}_{j}\hat{u}_{j}
, for
j=r,r+1,\dots,s
. Then

\displaystyle||\sin(\Theta)(V,\hat{V})||_{F}\leq\frac{2\left(2\sigma_{1}+||A-\hat{A}||_{2}\right)\min\{\sqrt{d}||A-\hat{A}||_{2},||A-\hat{A}||_{F}\}}{\min\{\sigma_{r-1}^{2}-\sigma_{r}^{2},\sigma_{s}^{2}-\sigma_{s+1}^{2}\}}.

Moreover, there exists an orthogonal matrix
\hat{Q}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
such that

\displaystyle||\hat{V}\hat{Q}-V||_{F}\leq\frac{2^{3/2}\left(2\sigma_{1}+||A-\hat{A}||_{2}\right)\min\{\sqrt{d}||A-\hat{A}||_{2},||A-\hat{A}||_{F}\}}{\min\{\sigma_{r-1}^{2}-\sigma_{r}^{2},\sigma_{s}^{2}-\sigma_{s+1}^{2}\}}.

Next, we have a result from
[
66
]
:

Lemma 11

(Proposition 2.2 in Vu & Lei
[
66
]
)
.

Let
\mathbb{V}_{p,q}=\left\{A\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}|\ A^{T}A=I_{q}\right\}
. If
V_{1},V_{2}\in\mathbb{V}_{p,q}
, then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\inf\limits_{Q\in\mathbb{V}_{q,q}}||V_{1}-V_{2}Q||_{F}^{2}\leq||\sin(\Theta)(V_{1},V_{2})||_{F}^{2}\leq\inf\limits_{Q\in\mathbb{V}_{q,q}}||V_{1}-V_{2}Q||_{F}^{2}.

A.2
Random Gaussian vectors

Recall the notion of a sub-Gaussian norm of a random variable:

Definition 14

(Definition 2.5 in
[
64
]
)
.

Let
X
be a sub-Gaussian random variable. The sub-Gaussian norm of X, denoted by
||X||_{\psi^{2}}
is defined as

\displaystyle||X||_{\psi^{2}}=\inf\left\{t>0|\ \mathbb{E}\left[e^{X^{2}/t^{2}}\right]\leq 2\right\}.

Remark 17
.

Note that if
X\sim N(0,\sigma^{2})
, then
||X||_{\psi^{2}}=C\sigma
, for some absolute constant
C>0
.

Lemma 12

(Corollary 2.7 in
[
64
]
)
.

Let
A
be an
f\times\ell
matrix, whose columns
A_{i}
are independent random vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{f}
from
N(0,\Sigma)
, with
\Sigma\succ 0
. Then for any
t\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle(\sqrt{\ell}-CK^{2}(\sqrt{f}+t))\sqrt{\sigma_{n}(\Sigma)}\leq\sigma_{n}(A)\leq(\sqrt{\ell}+CK^{2}(\sqrt{f}+t))\sqrt{\sigma_{n}(\Sigma)},

and

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}(A)\leq(\sqrt{\ell}+CK^{2}(\sqrt{f}+t))\sqrt{\sigma_{1}(\Sigma)},

with probability at least
1-2e^{-t^{2}}
, where
K:=\max\limits_{i,j}||A_{ij}||_{\psi^{2}}
.

Now we have a lemma regarding uniform concentration of Gaussian vectors:

Lemma 13

(Lemma 2.10 in
[
64
]
)
.

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{q}\sim N(0,\Sigma)
be vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
\Sigma
is the projection of
I_{d}
onto a subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
of rank
k
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\forall i,||X_{i}||_{2}^{2}\leq k+2\sqrt{kt}+2t\right)\geq 1-qe^{-t}.

A.3
Concentration inequalities

We begin by stating a concentration bound for sums of indicator variables:

Lemma 14

(Theorem
3
in
[
39
]
)
.

Let
X=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}
, where
X_{i}\overset{i.i.d.}{\sim}\mathrm{Ber}(p_{i})
. Let
\sigma^{2}=\mathrm{Var}(X)
. If
t\geq 0
, then

\displaystyle\max\left\{\mathbb{P}(X-\mathbb{E}[X]\geq t),\mathbb{P}(X-\mathbb{E}[X]\leq-t)\right\}\leq e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\left(1-\frac{t}{3\sigma^{2}}\right)}.

We now present a concentration result for hypergeometric random variables:

Lemma 15

(
[
36
]
)
.

Let
X\sim\mathrm{HG}(N,K,s)
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(X-\mathbb{E}[X]\geq ts),\mathbb{P}(X-\mathbb{E}[X]\leq-ts)\leq e^{-2t^{2}s},

for any
t>0
.

Remark 18
.

Consider Lemma
15
. Let
X\sim\mathrm{HG}(N,K,s)
. Then
\mathbb{P}(X=k)
is the probability of obtaining
k
successes in
s
draws, without replacement, from a population of size
N
, with
K
available successes.

Now we state a result concerning sub-Gaussian matrices:

Definition 15

(
[
67
]
)
.

A zero-mean, symmetric random matrix
Q\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
is sub-Gaussian with
matrix parameter
V\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
, where
V
is symmetric positive-definite, if

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\lambda Q}\right]:=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda^{i}}{i!}\mathbb{E}\left[Q^{i}\right]\preceq e^{\frac{\lambda^{2}V}{2}},

for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.

Next, we have a concentration result for sums of random matrices:

Lemma 16

(Hoeffding bound for random matrices
[
67
]
)
.

Let
\{Q_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
be a sequence of zero-mean,
m\times m
, independent, symmetric random matrices that satisfy the sub-Gaussian condition with parameters
\{V_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
. For all
\zeta>0
, we have the upper tail bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}Q_{i}\right\|_{2}\geq\zeta\right)\leq 2me^{-\frac{\zeta^{2}}{2\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}V_{i}\right\|_{2}}}.

We also have a result regarding the product of a random variable with a deterministic matrix:

Lemma 17

(Exercise 6.7 in
[
67
]
)
.

Let
g\in\mathbb{R}
be a zero-mean
s^{2}
-sub-Gaussian variable, with a distribution symmetric around
0
. Assume
B\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
is symmetric and deterministic. Then
gB
is sub-Gaussian with matrix parameter
c^{2}s^{2}B^{2}
, where
c
is a universal constant.

A.4
Privacy results

Following
[
25
]
, we formally define adaptive composition of differentially private mechanisms. Suppose
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i=T}
are mechanisms applied to a common database
\mathcal{X}^{n}
, such that
\mathcal{A}_{1}:\mathcal{X}^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{1}
, and for
i>1
, we have
\mathcal{A}_{i}:\mathcal{Y}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{Y}_{i-1}\times\mathcal{X}^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{i}
. The composition of
(\mathcal{A}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{A}_{T})
is the algorithm that maps
x\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
to

\mathcal{A}_{T}\Big(\mathcal{A}_{1}(x),\mathcal{A}_{2}\big(\mathcal{A}_{1}(x),x\big),\dots,x\Big).

Assuming that each
\mathcal{A}_{i}
is
(\epsilon_{i},\delta_{i})
-DP, in the sense that
\mathcal{A}_{i}(a_{1},\dots,a_{i-1},\cdot)
is
(\epsilon_{i},\delta_{i})
-DP for any fixed
(a_{1},\dots,a_{i-1})
, we wish to study the privacy properties of the composed mechanism.

Lemma 18

(Basic composition
[
24
]
)
.

Suppose each
\mathcal{A}_{i}
satisfies
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP, where
\epsilon>0
and
\delta\in[0,1]
. Then the composed mechanism satisfies
(T\epsilon,T\delta)
-DP.

If we introduce an extra slack term
\tilde{\delta}
, we can achieve a better bound in terms of the growth of
\epsilon
:

Lemma 19

(Adaptive composition
[
41
]
)
.

Suppose each
\mathcal{A}_{i}
satisfies
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP, where
\epsilon>0
and
\delta\in[0,1]
. Suppose
\tilde{\delta}\in(0,1]
. Then the composed mechanism satisfies
(\tilde{\epsilon}_{\tilde{\delta}},T\delta+\tilde{\delta})
-DP, where

\displaystyle\tilde{\epsilon}_{\tilde{\delta}}=T\epsilon(e^{\epsilon}-1)+\epsilon\sqrt{2T\log(1/\tilde{\delta})}.

In particular,
\tilde{\epsilon}_{\tilde{\delta}}=O(T\epsilon(e^{\epsilon}-1)+\epsilon\sqrt{T})
.

Lemma 20

(Group privacy
[
65
]
)
.

If
\mathcal{A}
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP, then
\mathcal{A}
is
(T\epsilon,T\delta e^{(T-1)\epsilon})
-DP, on groups of size at most
T
.

We now state a few results related to zero-concentrated differential privacy:

Lemma 21

(zCDP to DP from
[
11
]
)
.

If
\mathcal{A}
is
\rho
-zCDP, then it is
\left(\rho+\sqrt{4\rho\log(1/\delta)},\delta\right)
-DP, for any
\delta\in(0,1)
.

Lemma 22

(Composition of zCDP from
[
11
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}:\mathcal{X}^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}:\mathcal{X}^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}
be randomized algorithms. Suppose
\mathcal{M}
satisfies
\rho
-zCDP and
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
satisfies
\rho^{\prime}
-zCDP. Define
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}:\mathcal{X}^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z}
by
\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}(X)=(\mathcal{M}(X),\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(X))
. Then
\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}
satisfies
(\rho+\rho^{\prime})
-zCDP.

Remark 19
.

Note that by
[
69
]
, the same composition guarantee from Lemma
22
holds for fully-adaptive composition of zCDP mechanisms.

Lemma 23

(Postprocessing of zCDP from
[
11
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}:\mathcal{X}^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}
and
f:\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}
be randomized algorithms. Suppose
\mathcal{M}
satisfies
\rho
-zCDP. Define
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}:\mathcal{X}^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}
by
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}(X)=f(\mathcal{M}(X))
. Then
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
satisfies
\rho
-zCDP.

Lemma 24

(Group privacy of zCDP from
[
11
]
)
.

If
\mathcal{A}
is
\rho
-zCDP, it is
T^{2}\rho
-zCDP on groups of size
T
.

Lemma 25

(Gaussian mechanism for zCDP from
[
11
]
)
.

Let
f:\mathcal{X}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
have
\ell_{2}
-sensitivity
\Delta_{f}
. Consider the mechanism
\mathcal{M}:\mathcal{X}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which on input
X
, releases a sample from
N(f(X),\sigma^{2}I_{d})
, where
\sigma^{2}=\frac{\Delta_{f}^{2}}{2\rho}
. Then
\mathcal{M}
satisfies
\rho
-zCDP.

We also mention the exponential mechanism
[
53
]
:

Lemma 26

(Exponential mechanism
[
24
]
)
.

Consider a utility function
u:\mathbb{N}^{|\mathcal{X}|}\times\mathcal{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, which maps database/output pairs to utility scores, and suppose

\Delta u:=\max_{r\in\mathcal{R}}\max_{x,y:\|x-y\|_{1}\leq 1}|u(x,r)-u(y,r)|<\infty.

The mechanism
\mathcal{M}(x;u,\mathcal{R})
, which selects and outputs an element
r\in\mathcal{R}
with probability proportional to
\exp\left(\frac{\epsilon u(x,r)}{2\Delta u}\right)
, satisfies
(\epsilon,0)
-DP.

A.5
Properties of SBMs

We will use the following result about the deviation of the adjacency matrix in a random graph from its mean:

Lemma 27

(Theorem 5.2 in
[
50
]
)
.

Let
A
be the adjacency matrix of a random graph on
n
nodes in which edges occur independently. Suppose
n\cdot\max\limits_{i,j}\mathbb{E}[A]_{ij}\leq d
, for
d\geq c_{0}\log(n)
. Then for any
r>0
, there exists a constant
C=C(c_{0},r)
such that

\displaystyle||A-\mathbb{E}[A]||_{2}\leq C\sqrt{d},

with probability at least
1-n^{-r}
.

We will also use the following result, proved easily using basic concentration inequalities:

Lemma 28
.

Suppose
A\sim SBM(n,k,B,\theta)
, with parameters as in Assumption
1
.
For each
i\in[n]
, let
d_{i}=\|Ae_{i}\|_{1}
be the degree of node
i
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(d_{i}<2d,\quad\mathrm{for\ all}\ i\right)\geq 1-\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}.

Proof.

Let
\Omega_{0}=\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\left\{d_{i}<2\mathbb{E}[d_{i}]\right\}
. Let
j(i)\in[k]
be the community containing node
i
. For each
i
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[d_{i}]=\left(\frac{n}{k}-1\right)a_{n}(B_{0})_{j(i)j(i)}+\sum_{\ell\neq j(i)}\frac{na_{n}(B_{0})_{j(i)\ell}}{k},

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}^{2}=\text{Var}(d_{i})=\left(\frac{n}{k}-1\right)a_{n}(B_{0})+{j(i)j(i)}(1-a_{n}(B_{0})_{j(i)j(i)})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\sum_{\ell\neq j(i)}\frac{n}{k}a_{n}(B_{0})_{j(i)\ell}(1-a_{n}(B_{0})_{j(i)\ell}).

Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{0}^{c})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{P}(d_{i}\geq 2\mathbb{E}[d_{i}])\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{P}(d_{i}-\mathbb{E}[d_{i}]\geq\mathbb{E}[d_{i}])\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}e^{-\frac{\mathbb{E}[d_{i}]^{2}}{2\sigma_{i}^{2}}\left(1-\frac{\mathbb{E}[d_{i}]}{3\sigma_{i}^{2}}\right)},

using Lemma
14
to bound each summand, since
d_{i}
is a sum of i.i.d. Bernoulli random variables.
Under Assumption
1
, we have
\frac{\mathbb{E}[d_{i}]}{\sigma_{i}^{2}}\rightarrow 1
, as
n\rightarrow\infty
, for each
i
. Hence, for
n
sufficiently large, we have

e^{-\frac{\mathbb{E}[d_{i}]^{2}}{2\sigma_{i}^{2}}\left(1-\frac{\mathbb{E}[d_{i}]}{3\sigma_{i}^{2}}\right)}<e^{-\frac{\mathbb{E}[d_{i}]}{12}}
, for each
i
. Also, since
a_{n}\geq\frac{C_{1}k\log(n)}{n\cdot\text{min}_{j}{(B_{0})_{jj}}}
, we have

\mathbb{E}[d_{i}]\geq\left(\frac{n}{k}-1\right)a_{n}\cdot\text{min}_{j}{(B_{0})_{jj}}\geq C_{1}\log(n)-\frac{C_{1}k\log(n)}{n}\geq C_{1}\log(n)-C_{1}k,

for
n
sufficiently large, implying that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{0}^{c})<ne^{-\frac{C_{1}\log(n)}{12}+\frac{C_{1}k}{12}}\precsim n^{1-\frac{C_{1}}{12}}+n^{1-\frac{C_{1}}{24}}=o(1),

since
C_{1}>24
. Finally, for
i\in[n]
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[d_{i}]=\left(\frac{n}{k}-1\right)a_{n}(B_{0})_{j(i)j(i)}+\sum_{l\neq j(i)}\frac{na_{n}(B_{0})_{j(i)l}}{k}\leq(n-1)a_{n}\cdot\text{max}(B_{0})\leq d,

implying that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(d_{i}<2d,\quad\text{for all }i\right)\geq 1-\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)},

as required.
∎

A.6
Maximal correlation

Recall that the Hirschfeld-Gebelein-Rényi (HGR) maximal correlation is defined for random variables
(X,Y)
as

\rho_{m}(X,Y)=\max_{(f(X),g(Y))\in\mathcal{S}}\mathbb{E}[f(X)g(Y)],

where
\mathcal{S}
consists of pairs of random variables
f(X)
and
g(Y)
satisfying
\mathbb{E}[f(X)]=0=\mathbb{E}[g(Y)]
and
\mathbb{E}[f^{2}(X)]=1=\mathbb{E}[g^{2}(Y)]

[
2
,
60
]
. Clearly, by taking
f
and
g
to be appropriate linear transformations, we have
\rho_{m}(X,Y)\leq\rho(X,Y)
, where
\rho
is the usual Pearson correlation. Furthermore, when
X
and
Y
are Bernoulli, we have
\rho_{m}(X,Y)=\rho(X,Y)
, since all functions of Bernoulli variables are linear.

It is known that
\rho_{m}
tensorizes
[
70
]
, in the sense that the maximal correlation of two product distributions is bounded by the maximal correlation between pairs of coordinates:

Lemma 29
.

If
(X_{1},Y_{1})
and
(X_{2},Y_{2})
are independent, then

\rho_{m}\left((X_{1},X_{2}),(Y_{1},Y_{2})\right)=\max\left\{\rho_{m}(X_{1},Y_{1}),\rho_{m}(X_{2},Y_{2})\right\}.

Furthermore, it is evident from the definition that
\rho_{m}
satisfies a data processing inequality, so
\rho_{m}(f(X),g(Y))\leq\rho_{m}(X,Y)
.

Lemma 30
.

Suppose we have independent random variables
(Z,R_{1},R_{2})
, such that
Z\sim\mathrm{Ber}(q)
and
R_{1},R_{2}\sim\mathrm{Ber}(p)
. Then

\rho_{m}(ZR_{1},ZR_{2})=\frac{p(1-q)}{1-pq}.

Proof.

Since the maximal correlation
\rho_{m}
is equal to the Pearson correlation
\rho
for Bernoulli varaibles, we may simply calculate

\displaystyle\rho(ZR_{1},ZR_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{\operatorname{Cov}(ZR_{1},ZR_{2})}{\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}(ZR_{1})\operatorname{Var}(ZR_{2})}}=\frac{\mathbb{E}[Z^{2}R_{1}R_{2}]-\mathbb{E}[ZR_{1}]\mathbb{E}[ZR_{2}]}{pq(1-pq)}

\displaystyle=\frac{qp^{2}-p^{2}q^{2}}{pq(1-pq)}=\frac{p(1-q)}{1-pq},

as wanted.
∎

Appendix B
Details of privacy algorithms

In this appendix, we provide detailed statements of some of the privacy algorithms that were omitted in the main text due to space constraints.

B.1
Two-community private PCA with Lipschitz extension

We first describe the details of the algorithm studied in Section
4.3.1
(see Algorithm
4
).

Algorithm 4

Private PCA via Lipschitz Extension

1:

function

\mathcal{A}_{PLE}^{(\epsilon)}
(adjacency matrix
A\in\{0,1\}^{n\times n}
based on
G\in\mathcal{G}_{n}
, privacy parameter
\epsilon
, truncation parameter
D
, approximation error
\gamma
)

2:

Sample
L_{\epsilon}\sim\mathrm{Lap}\left(\frac{2}{\epsilon}\right)
, and compute
\widehat{\sigma}=\left[\frac{\textbf{1}_{n}^{T}A\textbf{1}_{n}}{n}+L_{\epsilon}\right]_{0}^{n}
.

3:

Sample
\hat{u}^{0}_{2}
from
\exp\left(\frac{\epsilon}{6D^{2}}\widehat{s}^{\prime}_{A^{2}}(v)\right)
w.r.t. the uniform measure on the unit sphere, where
\widehat{s}^{\prime}_{A^{2}}(v):=\widehat{s}_{A^{2}}(v)-\frac{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}}{n}v^{T}J_{n}v
.

4:

Set
\hat{u}_{2}=\frac{(I_{n}-J_{n}/n)\hat{u}^{0}_{2}}{||(I_{n}-J_{n}/n)\hat{u}^{0}_{2}||_{2}}
, so that
\hat{u}_{2}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{n}=0
.

5:

Construct
\hat{U}=\left[\frac{\mathbf{1}_{n}}{\sqrt{n}},\hat{u}_{2}\right]\in\mathbb{O}(n,2)
.

6:

Let
\hat{\Theta}\in\mathbf{M}_{n\times 2}
and
\hat{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times 2}
be such that

\displaystyle||\hat{\Theta}\hat{X}-\hat{U}||_{F}^{2}\leq(1+\gamma)\mathop{\inf}\limits_{\Theta^{\prime}\in\mathbf{M}_{n\times 2},X^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}}||\Theta^{\prime}X^{\prime}-\hat{U}||_{F}^{2}.

7:

Let
\hat{\theta}=\{\hat{\theta}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\in[2]^{n}
be given by
\hat{\theta}_{i}=\mathop{\arg\max}\limits_{j\in[2]}\hat{\Theta}_{ij}
, for all
i\in[n]
.

8:

Return
\hat{\theta}
.

9:

end

function

B.2
Clustering by edge flipping

We now provide the details of the spectral clustering algorithm from
[
35
]
, which applies spectral clustering after flipping the entries of the observed adjacency matrix.

Definition 16

(Symmetric edge-flip mechanism
[
35
]
)
.

Let
\epsilon>0
and let
A\in\{0,1\}^{n\times n}
be any adjacency matrix. For
i\in[n]
, let
A_{i*}
denote the
i^{\text{th}}
row of
A
, and let
\mathcal{M}_{i}:\{0,1\}^{n}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
be such that

\displaystyle[\mathcal{M}_{i}(x)]_{j}=\begin{cases}0&if\quad i\geq j,\\
1-x_{j}&if\quad i<j,\quad\textrm{with probability}\quad\frac{1}{1+e^{\epsilon}},\\
x_{j}&if\quad i<j,\quad\textrm{with probability}\quad\frac{e^{\epsilon}}{1+e^{\epsilon}}.\end{cases}

Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(A)

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{M}_{1}(A_{1*})\\
\vdots\\
\mathcal{M}_{n}(A_{n*})\end{bmatrix}.

Then the
n\times n
symmetric edge-flipping mechanism is
\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}(A)=\mathcal{T}(A)+\mathcal{T}(A)^{T}
.

Next, we present the private spectral clustering algorithm from
[
35
]
(see Algorithm
5
).

Algorithm 5

EF Spectral Clustering (k-means) from
[
35
]

1:

function

\mathcal{A}_{EF}^{(\epsilon)}
(adjacency matrix
A\in\{0,1\}^{n\times n}
based on
G\in\mathcal{G}_{n}
, number of communities
k
, approximation error
\gamma
, privacy budget
\epsilon
)

2:

Construct the edge-flipped
\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}(A)
.

3:

Let
A_{\downarrow}=\begin{cases}\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}(A)&if\quad\epsilon=\infty,\\
\mathcal{M}_{\epsilon}(A)-(e^{\epsilon}+1)^{-1}(\textbf{1}\textbf{1}^{T}-I_{n})&if\quad\epsilon<\infty.\end{cases}

4:

Let
\hat{u}_{1},\dots,\hat{u}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the
k
leading eigenvectors (by absolute value) of
A_{\downarrow}
.

5:

Let
\hat{\Theta}\in\mathbf{M}_{n\times k}
and
\hat{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
be such that

\displaystyle||\hat{\Theta}\hat{X}-U_{\downarrow}||_{F}^{2}\leq(1+\gamma)\mathop{\inf}\limits_{\Theta^{\prime}\in\mathbf{M}_{n\times k},X^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}}||\Theta^{\prime}X^{\prime}-U_{\downarrow}||_{F}^{2},

where
U_{\downarrow}=[\hat{u}_{1},\dots,\hat{u}_{k}]\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
.

6:

Let
\hat{\theta}=\{\hat{\theta}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\in[k]^{n}
be given by
\hat{\theta}_{i}=\mathop{\arg\max}\limits_{j\in[k]}\hat{\Theta}_{ij}
, for all
i\in[n]
.

7:

Return
\hat{\theta}
.

8:

end

function

B.3
Clustering by convex relaxation

We now describe the private estimation algorithm based on optimization from
[
15
]
(see Algorithm
6
).

Algorithm 6

Two-community Private Weak Recovery for SBM

1:

function

\mathcal{A}_{TC}^{(\epsilon,\delta)}
(adjacency matrix
A\in\{0,1\}^{n\times n}
based on
G\in\mathcal{G}_{n}
, probability matrix
B
of the SBM, privacy parameters
\epsilon,\delta
)

2:

Construct the rescaled, recentered matrix
Y=\frac{2}{n(B_{11}-B_{12})}\left(A-\frac{(B_{11}+B_{12})}{n}J_{n}\right)
.

3:

Solve the convex optimization problem

\widehat{X}\in\arg\min_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}:X\succeq 0,X_{ii}=\frac{1}{n}\;\forall i}\|Y-X\|_{F}^{2}.

4:

Compute the leading unit eigenvector
\widetilde{x}
of
\widehat{X}+N
, where
N\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is a matrix of i.i.d. entries distributed as
N\left(0,\frac{96\log(1/\delta)}{n^{2}\epsilon^{2}(B_{11}-B_{12})}\right)
.

5:

For each
i\in[n]
, assign node
i
to community
\text{sign}(\widetilde{x}_{i})
. Call this
\hat{\theta}\in[2]^{n}
.

6:

Return
\hat{\theta}
.

7:

end

function

B.4
Clustering by low-rank matrix estimation

We now provide the private spectral clustering method based on private low-rank matrix estimation, as studied in
[
33
,
28
]
(see Algorithm
7
).

Algorithm 7

Spectral Clustering via Matrix Estimation

1:

function

\mathcal{A}_{ME}^{(\epsilon,\delta)}
(adjacency matrix
A\in\{0,1\}^{n\times n}
based on
G\in\mathcal{G}_{n}
, iteration count
L
, number of communities
k
, approximation error
\gamma
, privacy parameters
\epsilon,\delta
)

2:

Start running PPM from
[
33
]
: Let
X_{0}
be a random orthonormal basis.

3:

for

l=1,\dots,L

do

4:

Y_{l}=AX_{l-1}+G_{l}
, where
G_{l}\sim N\left(0,\frac{4kL\log(1/\delta)}{\epsilon^{2}}\right)^{n\times 2k}
.

5:

Compute the QR factorization
Y_{l}=X_{l}R_{l}
.

6:

end

for

7:

Compute the rank-
2k
matrix
\hat{A}_{(2k)}=X_{L-1}Y_{L}^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
. This ends PPM from
[
33
]
.

8:

Compute the SVD:
\hat{A}_{(2k)}=U_{(2k)}D_{(2k)}V_{(2k)}^{T}
, with
U_{(2k)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times 2k}
. Extract the first
k
columns of
U_{(2k)}
, and denote them by
U_{(k)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
.

9:

Let
\hat{\Theta}\in\mathbf{M}_{n\times k}
and
\hat{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
be such that

\displaystyle||\hat{\Theta}\hat{X}-U_{(k)}||_{F}^{2}\leq(1+\gamma)\mathop{\inf}\limits_{\Theta^{\prime}\in\mathbf{M}_{n\times k},X^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}}||\Theta^{\prime}X^{\prime}-U_{(k)}||_{F}^{2}.

10:

Define
\hat{\theta}=\{\hat{\theta}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\in[k]^{n}
by
\hat{\theta}_{i}=\mathop{\arg\max}\limits_{j\in[k]}\hat{\Theta}_{ij}
, for all
i\in[n]
.

11:

Return
\hat{\theta}
.

12:

end

function

B.5
Clustering by approximate subspace estimation

Finally, we provide the details of the spectral clustering algorithm which uses a private approximate subspace method (some details are omitted, which relate to the algorithmic details in the earlier papers
[
64
,
42
,
52
]
). See Algorithm
8
.

Algorithm 8

Spectral Clustering via Subspace Estimation

1:

function

\mathcal{A}_{SE}^{(\epsilon,\delta)}
(adjacency matrix
A\in\{0,1\}^{n\times n}
based on
G\in\mathcal{G}_{n}
(or weighted adjacency matrix
W\odot A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
based on
G\in\mathcal{W}_{n}
), number of communities
k\asymp 1
, approximation error
\gamma
, number of Gaussian points
q=C^{\prime}k
(can choose
0<C^{\prime}\asymp 1
as large as we like), privacy parameters
\epsilon,\delta
, failure error
1\asymp\zeta\in(0,1/3)
s.t.
3k\geq\log(2/\zeta)
, parameters
\rho=\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{32q\log(1/\delta)}
,
R_{max}=\sqrt{n}\log(n)
,
r_{min}=\frac{1}{n^{3}}
)

2:

Randomly divide the rows of
A
(or
W\odot A
) into
t
chunks
\{A_{1},\dots,A_{t}\}
(or
\{(W\odot A)_{1},\dots,(W\odot A)_{t}\}
). Let
m=\frac{n}{t}
, so
A_{j},(W\odot A)_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
. That is, sample uniformly from the set of permutations of
[n]
, and split these permuted indexes sequentially into
t
groups of size
m
,

3:

Form
\Pi_{1},\dots,\Pi_{t}
.
\Pi_{j}=U_{j}^{(k)}(U_{j}^{(k)})^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
, where
A_{j}A_{j}^{T}=U_{j}D_{j}U_{j}^{T}
(similarly for
(W\odot A)_{j}(W\odot A)_{j}^{T}
), and
U_{j}^{(k)}=[U_{j}^{1},\dots,U_{j}^{k}]\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times k}
.

4:

Form
\Pi^{(1)},\dots,\Pi^{(t)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, where
\Pi^{(j)}
is the projection onto the
row
space of
\Pi_{j}A_{j}
(or
\Pi_{j}(W\odot A)_{j}
). Write
\Pi^{(j)}=U^{(j),(k)}(U^{(j),(k)})^{T}
, where
U^{(j),(k)}
consists of the first
k
columns from the matrix of orthonormal eigenvectors from the SVD of
(\Pi_{j}A_{j})^{T}\Pi_{j}A_{j}
(or
\Pi_{j}(W\odot A)_{j}^{T}(\Pi_{j}(W\odot A)_{j})
).

5:

Sample reference points
z_{1},\dots,z_{q}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d.}}{{\sim}}N(0,I_{n})
. Construct
z_{i}^{j}=\Pi^{(j)}z_{i}
.

6:

Let
X_{i}=\{z_{i}^{j,grid}\}_{j=1}^{t}
, where
z_{i}^{j,grid}
is the closest point (in
\ell_{2}
-norm) on the grid
\left[-\frac{R_{max}}{\sqrt{n}},\frac{R_{max}}{\sqrt{n}}\right]^{n}
with spacing
r_{min}
, to
z_{i}^{j}
.

7:

For each
i
, run
\mathrm{GoodCenter}_{X_{i},0,R_{max},\frac{r_{min}}{2},\frac{\zeta}{q},\rho}
to obtain
\theta^{*}_{i}
.

8:

Compute
r=\frac{\sqrt{\log(n)}}{n^{2}\sqrt{n}}+\left(\frac{\log^{1/4}(1/\delta)}{\log^{5/2}(n)\sqrt{\epsilon}}+\frac{\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}}{\log^{5}(n)\epsilon}\right)\log(n)
.

9:

For each
i
, truncate all
z_{i}^{j}
’s to the ball
B(\theta^{*}_{i},r)
. Let
\hat{z}_{i}=\frac{1}{t}\sum_{j=1}^{t}z_{i}^{j}+N(0,\sigma^{2}I_{n})
, where
\sigma=\frac{2r}{t\sqrt{2\rho}}
.

10:

Let
\hat{Z}=[\hat{z}_{1},\dots,\hat{z}_{q}]\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times q}
. Write the SVD of
\hat{Z}=\hat{U}\hat{D}\hat{V}^{T}
.
Extract
\hat{U}^{(k)}=[\hat{U}_{1},\dots,\hat{U}_{k}]\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
. Form the projection
\hat{\Pi}
.

11:

Let
\hat{\Theta}\in\mathbf{M}_{n\times k}
and
\hat{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
be such that

\displaystyle||\hat{\Theta}\hat{X}-\hat{U}^{(k)}||_{F}^{2}\leq(1+\gamma)\mathop{\inf}\limits_{\Theta^{\prime}\in\mathbf{M}_{n\times k},X^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}}||\Theta^{\prime}X^{\prime}-\hat{U}^{(k)}||_{F}^{2}.

12:

Let
\hat{\theta}=\{\hat{\theta}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\in[k]^{n}
be given by
\hat{\theta}_{i}=\mathop{\arg\max}\limits_{j\in[k]}\hat{\Theta}_{ij}
, for all
i\in[n]
.

13:

Return
\hat{\theta}
.

14:

end

function

We also present Algorithm 6 from the supplement of
[
52
]
and its corresponding utility guarantee (see Algorithm
9
).

Algorithm 9

Noisy Average and Radius (GoodCenter)

1:

function

GoodCenter
(set of
t
points
X\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times t}
, parameters
\theta_{0}
,
R_{max}
, and
r_{min}
, such that
X\subset B(\theta_{0},R_{max})
, and
r_{opt}\geq r_{min}
, where
r_{opt}
is the radius of the minimum enclosing ball of
X
. Failure parameter
\zeta\in(0,1)
, privacy parameter
\rho
.)

2:

Set
T=\lceil\log_{2}(R_{max}/r_{min})\rceil+1
,
Y=\sqrt{\frac{2T\log(4T/\zeta)}{\rho}}
.

3:

Set
\sigma_{count}^{2},\sigma_{sum}^{2}=\frac{T}{\rho}
.

4:

Initialize
X_{0}\leftarrow X
,
\theta^{0}\leftarrow\theta_{0}
,
n_{cur}\leftarrow n
, and
r_{cur}\leftarrow R_{max}
.

5:

for

s=0,\dots,S-1

do

6:

X^{s}\leftarrow X^{s}\cap B(\theta^{s},r_{cur})
.

7:

Sample
\Delta_{sum}\sim N(0,4r_{cur}^{2}\sigma_{sum}^{2}I_{n})
.

8:

\tilde{\mu}^{s}\leftarrow\left(\mathop{\sum}\limits_{x\in X^{s}}x+\Delta_{sum}\right)/n_{cur}
.

9:

\Delta_{count}=N(0,\sigma_{count}^{2})
.

10:

if

\left|X^{s}\setminus B\left(\tilde{\mu}^{s},\frac{r_{cur}}{2}\right)\right|+\Delta_{count}\geq Y

then

11:

Return
B(\theta^{s},r_{cur})
.

12:

end

if

13:

Update:
r_{cur}\leftarrow\frac{r_{cur}}{2}
,
n_{cur}\leftarrow n_{cur}-2Y
,
\theta^{s+1}\leftarrow\tilde{\mu}^{s}
.

14:

end

for

15:

Return
B(\theta^{S},r_{cur})
.

16:

end

function

Lemma 31

(Corollary A.3 in
[
52
]
)
.

Let
X=[X_{1},\dots,X_{t}]^{n\times t}
and
\zeta\in(0,1)
. Suppose the
t
points lie on a grid in
[-B,B]^{n\times n}
of step size
\tau
. Then
\mathrm{GoodCenter}_{X,0,B\sqrt{n},\frac{\tau}{2},\zeta,\rho}
(Algorithm
9
) is
\rho
-zCDP (with respect to a change among the
t
points). In addition, for

\displaystyle t\succsim\sqrt{\frac{\log(Bn/\tau)}{\rho}}\left(\sqrt{n}+\sqrt{\log(Bn/(\tau\zeta))}\right).

\mathrm{GoodCenter}_{X,0,B\sqrt{n},\frac{\tau}{2},\zeta,\rho}
returns, with probability at least
1-\zeta
, a ball
B(\theta^{*},r^{*})
satisfying: For
X^{\prime}=X\setminus B(\theta^{*},r^{*})
, it holds
|X^{\prime}|\geq\frac{t}{2}
. For
B(\theta_{opt}(X^{\prime}),r_{opt}(X^{\prime}))
the smallest
\ell_{2}
-ball in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
enclosing
X^{\prime}
, we have
||\theta^{*}-\theta_{opt}(X^{\prime})||_{2}\leq 6r_{opt}(X^{\prime})
.

Now we state and prove a matrix estimation result based on
[
33
]
:

Lemma 32

(Adapted from Corollary 4.6 in
[
33
]
)
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be an adjacency matrix, and let
\gamma_{1}=1-\frac{\sigma_{k+1}(A)}{\sigma_{k}(A)}
. There exists an efficient algorithm (cf.
\hat{A}_{(2k)}
in Algorithm
7
) that, given
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
and
k
, returns a rank-
2k
matrix
\hat{A}_{(2k)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
such that, with probability at least
0.9
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|A-\hat{A}_{(2k)}\right\|_{2}\leq\sigma_{k+1}(A)+O\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}(A)\sqrt{(k/\gamma_{1})n\log(n)\log(\log(n)/\gamma_{1})}}{\sigma_{k}(A)-\sigma_{k+1}(A)}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}}{\epsilon}\right).

In addition, the algorithm is
\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{4\log(1/\delta)}
-edge zCDP.

Proof.

We start with the privacy guarantee. Consider the mechanism PPM from
[
33
]
(see Algorithm
7
), executed with each
G_{l}
sampled independently as
G_{l}\sim N(0,\sigma^{2})^{n\times p}
, with
\sigma^{2}=\frac{4kL\log(1/\delta)}{\epsilon^{2}}
. Let
A
and
A^{\prime}
be edge-adjacent and differing in entries
(i,j)
and
(j,i)
. Then
(A-A^{\prime})(A-A^{\prime})^{T}
is the diagonal matrix with a
1
in entries
(i,i)
and
(j,j)
, and
0
everywhere else. Hence, we have
||A-A^{\prime}||_{2}\leq 1
. At step
l
of the algorithm, for fixed
X_{l-1}
, the
\ell_{2}
-sensitivity of
AX_{l-1,s}
, where
X_{l-1,s}
, the
s^{\text{th}}
column of
X_{l-1}
, is
1
. Since we have
2k
columns and
L
iterations in total, using the Gaussian mechanism from Lemma
25
and the composition of zCDP mechanisms for Lemma
22
(and the adaptive version), we obtain the desired privacy guarantee.

For the utility analysis, choose
L\asymp\log(n)/\gamma_{1}
. Following the lines of the proof of Corollary 4.6 in
[
33
]
, we obtain the desired utility.
∎

Appendix C
Proofs for Section
4

C.1
Proof of Lemma
1

Note that

\mathrm{Tr}(V^{T}A^{2}V)=\|AV\|_{F}^{2},\qquad\mathrm{Tr}(V^{T}(A^{\prime})^{2}V)=\|A^{\prime}V\|_{F}^{2}.

Thus,

|\mathrm{Tr}(V^{T}A^{2}V)-\mathrm{Tr}(V^{T}(A^{\prime})^{2}V)|=\left|\|AV\|_{F}^{2}-\|A^{\prime}V\|_{F}^{2}\right|.

Furthermore,

\|AV\|_{F}^{2}\leq\|A\|_{2}^{2}\|V\|_{F}^{2}=q\|A\|_{2}^{2}\leq q\|A\|_{1}\|A\|_{\infty}\leq qD^{2},

and similarly for
\|A^{\prime}V\|_{F}^{2}
. Since both quantities are nonnegative, we see that

\left|\|AV\|_{F}^{2}-\|A^{\prime}V\|_{F}^{2}\right|\leq qD^{2},

as claimed.

C.2
Proof of Lemma
2

We will show that the global sensitivity of
\widehat{s}_{A^{2}}(v)
is bounded by
(q+2)D^{2}
. Consider two node-adjacent adjacency matrices
(A,A^{\prime})
, and note that any matrix
C
which is feasible in the LP corresponding to
\widehat{s}_{A^{2}}(v)
can be made into a matrix
C^{\prime}
feasible in the LP corresponding to
\widehat{s}_{A^{\prime 2}}(v)
by zeroing out at most one row and column. Note that

s_{C}(V)-s_{C^{\prime}}(V)=\mathrm{Tr}(V^{T}(C-C^{\prime})V)+\mathrm{Tr}((C-C^{\prime})J_{n}).

The latter term is the sum of the entries in a single row/column of
C-C^{\prime}
, and since
\|C\|_{\infty}\leq D^{2}
, it must be bounded by
2D^{2}
. Furthermore, consider
W=W^{T}
and
Z\succeq 0
, with spectral decomposition
W=HLH^{T}=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{i=1}^{n}L_{ii}H_{i}H_{i}^{T}
. Since
H_{i}ZH_{i}^{T}\geq 0
, for all
i\in[n]
, and

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}(WZ)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{Tr}(L_{ii}ZH_{i}H_{i}^{T})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{ii}H_{i}ZH_{i}^{T}\leq||W||_{2}\sum_{i}\mathrm{Tr}(HZH^{T})=||W||_{2}\mathrm{Tr}(Z),

we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}(V^{T}(C-C^{\prime})V)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}((C-C^{\prime})VV^{T})\leq\mathrm{Tr}(VV^{T})\|C-C^{\prime}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{Tr}(V^{T}V)\|C-C^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq q\|C\|_{\infty}\leq qD^{2}.

Altogether, we have

s_{C}(V)-s_{C^{\prime}}(V)\leq(q+2)D^{2},

so by taking a supremum over feasible matrices
C
, we clearly have

\widehat{s}_{A^{2}}(V)\leq\widehat{s}_{A^{\prime 2}}(V)+(q+2)D^{2}.

A similar inequality holds in the opposite direction, implying the desired sensitivity bound.

Applying the exponential mechanism to the score function
\widehat{s}_{A^{2}}(V)
then gives the desired result.

C.3
Proof of Corollary
1

Fix
M\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
. By the proof of Lemma
2
, we have
|\widehat{s}_{A^{2}}(V)-\widehat{s}_{A^{\prime 2}}(V)|\leq(q+2)D^{2}
. Since

|\widehat{s}_{A^{2}}(V)+V^{T}MV-\widehat{s}_{A^{\prime 2}}(V)-V^{T}MV|\leq(q+2)D^{2},

the conclusion follows.

C.4
Proof of Theorem
1

Regarding the privacy guarantee, note that
\frac{\textbf{1}_{n}^{T}A\textbf{1}_{n}}{n}
has sensitivity at most
2
, so
\widehat{\sigma}
is
(\epsilon,0)
-DP. Next, for any fixed
\sigma^{2}
, sampling from
\exp\left(\frac{\epsilon}{6D^{2}}(\widehat{s}_{A^{2}}(v)-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}v^{T}J_{n}v)\right)
is
(\epsilon,0)
-node DP, by Corollary
1
. Hence, by adaptive composition, the vector
\hat{u}_{2}^{0}
is
(2\epsilon,0)
-node DP, and by post-processing, the vector
\hat{u}_{2}
is
(2\epsilon,0)
-node DP, as well. Finally, by post-processing again, the matrix
\hat{U}
and all derived quantities satisfy
(2\epsilon,0)
-node DP.

Regarding utility, we have the following lemma, proved in Appendix
C.5.1
:

Lemma 33
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
1
, there exists an event
\Omega
with
\mathbb{P}(\Omega)\geq 1-\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}
, such that on
\Omega
, we have
||A-P||_{2}\precsim\sqrt{d}
,
|\widehat{\sigma}^{2}-\sigma_{1}^{2}(P)|\precsim d\sqrt{d}
, and
\widehat{s}_{A^{2}}(v)=s_{A^{2}}(v)
, for every
v
.

To reason about
\hat{u}_{2}^{0}
and
\hat{u}_{2}
, we use following lemma, proved in Appendix
C.5.2
:

Lemma 34
.

Let
M=A^{2}+n^{2}I_{n}-\frac{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}}{n}J_{n}
, and let
u_{2}
be a leading unit eigenvector of
M
. Then the vector
\hat{u}_{2}^{0}
sampled in Algorithm
4
satisfies
\mathbb{P}\left(|(\hat{u}_{2}^{0})^{T}u_{2}|^{2}>1-\frac{D^{2}}{\epsilon}\right)\geq 1-\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}
.

Let
1-\rho^{2}=\frac{D^{2}}{\epsilon}
, as in the proof of Lemma
34
, and let
\Omega
be as in Lemma
33
. Let
u_{2,P}
be the second orthonormal eigenvector of
P
. We will control
|u_{2,P}^{T}\hat{u}_{2}|
. First, let
u_{2}^{*}
be the top eigenvector of
A^{2}-\frac{\sigma_{1}^{2}(P)}{n}J_{n}
. We check the eigengap condition using the following result, proved in Appendix
C.5.3
:

Lemma 35
.

Let
A_{1}=A^{2}-\frac{\sigma_{1}^{2}}{n}\textbf{1}_{n}\textbf{1}_{n}^{T}
and
A_{0}=A^{2}-\frac{\widehat{\sigma}_{1}^{2}}{n}\textbf{1}_{n}\textbf{1}_{n}^{T}
. Let
u_{2}^{*}
be the top eigenvector of
A_{1}
, and let
u_{2}
be the top eigenvector of
A_{0}
. Then

\mathop{\inf}_{q:q^{2}=1}||qu_{2}-u_{2}^{*}||_{2}\precsim\frac{|\widehat{\sigma}_{1}^{2}-\sigma_{1}^{2}|}{\lambda_{1}(A_{1})-\lambda_{2}(A_{1})}.

On
\Omega
, we have
|\widehat{\sigma}^{2}-\sigma_{1}^{2}(P)|\precsim d\sqrt{d}
. Also, by Lemma
6
, we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}\left(A^{2}-\frac{\sigma_{1}^{2}(P)}{n}J_{n}\right)\geq\sigma_{2}^{2}(P)-||A^{2}-P^{2}||_{2}\succsim d^{2},

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}\left(A^{2}-\frac{\sigma_{1}^{2}(P)}{n}J_{n}\right)-\lambda_{2}\left(A^{2}-\frac{\sigma_{1}^{2}(P)}{n}J_{n}\right)\geq\sigma_{2}^{2}(P)-2||A^{2}-P^{2}||_{2}\succsim d^{2}.

Thus, by Lemma
35
, we have on
\Omega
that
||u_{2}-q_{1}u_{2}^{*}||_{2}\leq\frac{c_{1}}{\sqrt{d}}
, for some
0<c_{1}\asymp 1
, with
q_{1}^{2}=1
. If
q_{1}=-1
, we can replace
u_{2}^{*}
by
-u_{2}^{*}
, since we can pick any top unit singular vector of
A^{2}-\frac{\sigma_{1}^{2}(P)}{n}J_{n}
. We thus have
||u_{2}-u_{2}^{*}||_{2}\leq\frac{c_{1}}{\sqrt{d}}
. We now derive the following result, proved in Appendix
C.5.4
:

Lemma 36
.

Let
\frac{\mathbf{1}_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}
be the top eigenvector of
P
, with top eigenvalue given by
\sigma_{1}
. Let
A_{1}=A^{2}-\frac{\sigma_{1}^{2}}{n}\textbf{1}_{n}\textbf{1}_{n}^{T}
and
P_{1}=P^{2}-\frac{\sigma_{1}^{2}}{n}\textbf{1}_{n}\textbf{1}_{n}^{T}
. Let
u_{2}^{*}
be the top eigenvector of
A_{1}
, and let
u_{2,P}
be the top eigenvector of
P_{1}
. Then

\mathop{\inf}_{q:q^{2}=1}||qu_{2}^{*}-u_{2,P}||_{2}\precsim\frac{||A^{2}-P^{2}||_{2}}{\sigma_{2}^{2}(P)}.

By Lemma
36
, we have, on
\Omega
, that

\displaystyle\mathop{\inf}_{q:q^{2}=1}||qu_{2}^{*}-u_{2,P}||_{2}\precsim\frac{||A^{2}-P^{2}||_{2}}{\sigma_{2}^{2}(P)}\precsim\frac{d\sqrt{d}}{d^{2}}\precsim\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}.

Hence, on
\Omega
, we have
||u_{2}^{*}-q_{2}u_{2,P}||_{2}\leq\frac{c_{2}}{\sqrt{d}}
, for some
c_{2}\asymp 1
and
q_{2}^{2}=1
. If
q_{2}=1
, we can pick
-u_{2,P}
instead of
u_{2,P}
. Hence, we have
||u_{2}^{*}+u_{2,P}||_{2}\leq\frac{c_{2}}{\sqrt{d}}
. Thus, since

||(I_{n}-J_{n}/n)\hat{u}_{2}^{0}||_{2}\leq||I_{n}-J_{n}/n||_{2}||\hat{u}_{2}^{0}||_{2}\leq 1,

we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(|\hat{u}_{2}^{T}u_{2,P}|\leq\rho^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(|u_{2,P}^{T}(I_{n}-J_{n}/n)\hat{u}_{2}^{0}|\leq\rho^{\prime}\right)\leq\mathbb{P}\left(|(\hat{u}_{2}^{0})^{T}u_{2,P}|-|\hat{u}_{2}^{T}J_{n}u_{2,P}|/n\leq\rho^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(|(\hat{u}_{2}^{0})^{T}u_{2,P}|\leq\rho^{\prime}),

since
u_{2,P}
and
\frac{\mathbf{1}_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}
are the top orthonormal eigenvectors of
P
, so
u_{2,P}^{T}J_{n}=0
. Now, for
\rho^{\prime}=\rho-(c_{1}+c_{2})/\sqrt{d}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(|\hat{u}_{2}^{T}u_{2,P}|\leq\rho^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(|u_{2}^{T}\hat{u}_{2}^{0}|\leq\rho^{\prime}+|(u_{2}^{*}-u_{2})^{T}\hat{u}_{2}^{0}|+|(u_{2}^{*}+u_{2,P})^{T}\hat{u}_{2}^{0}|\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(|u_{2}^{T}\hat{u}_{2}^{0}|\leq\rho^{\prime}+||u_{2}^{*}-u_{2}||_{2}+||u_{2}^{*}+u_{2,P}||_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(|u_{2}^{T}\hat{u}_{2}^{0}|\leq\rho^{\prime}+||u_{2}^{*}-u_{2}||_{2}+||u_{2}^{*}+u_{2,P}||_{2}|\Omega\right)\mathbb{P}(\Omega)+\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(|u_{2}^{T}\hat{u}_{2}^{0}|\leq\rho^{\prime}+(c_{1}+c_{2})/\sqrt{d}|\Omega\right)\mathbb{P}(\Omega)+\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(|u_{2}^{T}\hat{u}_{2}^{0}|\leq\rho^{\prime}+(c_{1}+c_{2})/\sqrt{d}\right)+\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(|u_{2}^{T}\hat{u}_{2}^{0}|\leq\rho\right)+\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}\leq\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)},

by Lemma
34
. Let
U=\left[\mathbf{1}_{n}/\sqrt{n},u_{2,P}\right]
. Then

\displaystyle||\hat{U}\hat{U}^{T}-UU^{T}||_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=||\hat{u}_{2}\hat{u}_{2}^{T}-u_{2,P}u_{2,P}^{T}||_{2}^{2}\leq||\hat{u}_{2}\hat{u}_{2}^{T}-u_{2,P}u_{2,P}^{T}||_{F}^{2}=\mathrm{Tr}((\hat{u}_{2}\hat{u}_{2}^{T}-u_{2,P}u_{2,P}^{T})^{2})

\displaystyle=2-2(\hat{u}_{2}^{T}u_{2,P})^{2}\leq 2(1-(\rho^{\prime})^{2})\leq 2\left(1-\rho^{2}-\frac{(c_{1}+c_{2})^{2}}{d}+\frac{2\rho(c_{1}+c_{2})}{\sqrt{d}}\right)

\displaystyle\precsim\frac{D^{2}}{\epsilon}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}<\frac{1}{\log(n)},

with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}
, since
1>\rho^{2}=1-\frac{D^{2}}{\epsilon}>1-\frac{1}{\log(n)}
. Now note that by Lemma
8
and Lemma
11
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\inf\limits_{Q\in\mathbb{V}_{2,2}}||\hat{U}-UQ||_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}||\hat{U}-UQ^{\prime}||_{F}^{2}\leq||\sin(\Theta)(\hat{U},U)||_{F}^{2}\leq 2||\sin(\Theta)(\hat{U},U)||_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2||\hat{U}\hat{U}^{T}-UU^{T}||_{2}^{2}\precsim\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}+\frac{D^{2}}{\epsilon},

where
Q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
and
Q^{\prime T}Q^{\prime}=I_{2}
. The rest of the proof follows the lines of argument from the proof of Theorem 5.1 in
[
35
]
. We know from
[
50
]
that
U=\Theta X
, for some
\Theta\in\mathbf{M}_{n\times 2}
and
X
satisfying
||X_{j*}-X_{l*}||_{2}=\sqrt{\frac{4}{n}}
, for
j\neq l
. Then
UQ^{\prime}=\Theta XQ^{\prime}=\Theta X^{\prime}
, with
X^{\prime}=XQ^{\prime}
, and

||X^{\prime}_{j*}-X^{\prime}_{l*}||_{2}=||(e_{j}-e_{l})^{T}XQ^{\prime}||_{2}=||X_{j*}-X_{l*}||_{2}=\sqrt{\frac{4}{n}},

for
j\neq l
. Here,
e_{j}
is the
j^{\text{th}}
standard basis vector. For all
j\in[2]
, choose
\delta_{j}=\sqrt{\frac{4}{n}}
and define
S_{j}
as in Lemma 5.3 of
[
50
]
. We want to show that
(16+8\gamma)||\hat{U}-UQ^{\prime}||_{F}^{2}<\frac{\delta_{j}^{2}n}{2}
, for all
j\in[k]
. Since
\frac{\delta_{j}^{2}n}{2}>1
, it is enough to prove that
(16+8\gamma)||\hat{U}-UQ^{\prime}||_{F}^{2}\leq 1
. Let
c_{3}\asymp 1
be the absolute constant such that

\displaystyle c_{3}||\hat{U}-UQ^{\prime}||_{F}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}+\frac{D^{2}}{\epsilon}.

Then

\displaystyle(16+8\gamma)||\hat{U}-UQ^{\prime}||_{F}^{2}\leq\frac{16+8\gamma}{c_{3}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}+\frac{D^{2}}{\epsilon}\right)<\frac{16+8\gamma}{c_{3}\log(n)}\leq 1,

as desired, for
n
large enough, since
\gamma,c_{3}\asymp 1
. Thus, for each
community
j\in[2]
, the set of nodes that are possibly misclassified by Algorithm
4
must be a subset of
S_{j}
, and since
\frac{\delta_{j}^{2}n}{2}>1
, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{PLE}^{(\epsilon)}(G),\theta\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathop{\max}_{j\in[2]}\frac{2|S_{j}|}{n}\leq\frac{2}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{2}|S_{j}|\leq\sum_{j=1}^{2}|S_{j}|\delta_{j}^{2}\leq(16+8\gamma)||\hat{U}-UQ^{\prime}||_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{16+8\gamma}{c_{3}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}+\frac{D^{2}}{\epsilon}\right)\precsim\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}+\frac{D^{2}}{\epsilon}<\frac{1}{\log(n)},

with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}
, as desired, since
\gamma,c_{3}\asymp 1
.

C.5
Proofs of supporting lemmas for Theorem
1

We now provide the proofs of the lemmas stated above in the proof of Theorem
1
.

C.5.1
Proof of Lemma
33

Note that by Lemma
28
, there exists an event
\Omega_{0}
with
\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{0})\geq 1-\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}
, such that on
\Omega_{0}
, the degree of every node in
A
is at most
2d
. Hence, since
D\geq 3d
, we have
\widehat{s}_{A^{2}}(v)=s_{A^{2}}(v)
on
\Omega_{0}
. Next, by Lemma
27
, since
a_{n}\succsim\frac{\log(n)}{n}
, there is an event
\Omega_{1}
, with
\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{1})\geq 1-\frac{1}{n}
, such that

||A-\mathbb{E}[A]||_{2}=||A-P+\text{diag}(P)||_{2}\precsim\sqrt{na}\asymp\sqrt{d}

on
\Omega_{1}
. Next, for
\Omega_{2}=\left\{|L_{\epsilon}|\leq\frac{2\log(n)}{\epsilon}\right\}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\Omega_{2}^{c}\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(|\mathrm{Lap}(1)|>\log(n)\right)\leq\frac{2}{n}.

Hence,
|L_{\epsilon}|\leq\frac{2\log(n)}{\epsilon}<\frac{2}{D^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{n}
on
\Omega_{2}
, since
\epsilon>D^{2}\log(n)\geq d^{2}\log(n)>n\log(n)
. Let
\Omega=\Omega_{0}\cap\Omega_{1}\cap\Omega_{2}
, so
\mathbb{P}(\Omega)\geq 1-\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}
. Next, on
\Omega
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\textbf{1}_{n}^{T}A\textbf{1}_{n}}{n}+L_{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=\frac{\textbf{1}_{n}^{T}P\textbf{1}_{n}}{n}+\frac{\textbf{1}_{n}^{T}(A-P)\textbf{1}_{n}}{n}+L_{\epsilon}

\displaystyle\leq\sigma_{1}(P)+||A-P||_{2}+\frac{1}{n}\precsim d+\sqrt{d}+\frac{1}{n}\precsim d.

Also,

\displaystyle\frac{\textbf{1}_{n}^{T}A\textbf{1}_{n}}{n}+L_{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=\frac{\textbf{1}_{n}^{T}P\textbf{1}_{n}}{n}+\frac{\textbf{1}_{n}^{T}(A-P)\textbf{1}_{n}}{n}+L_{\epsilon}

\displaystyle\geq\sigma_{1}(P)-||A-P||_{2}-\frac{1}{n}\succsim d-\sqrt{d}-\frac{1}{n}\succsim d.

Since
0<d=o(n)
, we obtain
\widehat{\sigma}=\frac{\textbf{1}_{n}^{T}A\textbf{1}_{n}}{n}+L_{\epsilon}\asymp d
on
\Omega
. Moreover, we have

\displaystyle|\widehat{\sigma}^{2}-\sigma_{1}^{2}(P)|=|\widehat{\sigma}-\sigma_{1}(P)|\cdot|\widehat{\sigma}+\sigma_{1}(P)|\precsim d\sqrt{d},

completing the proof.

C.5.2
Proof of Lemma
34

Let
\Omega
be as in Lemma
33
. Note that
M\succeq 0
, since
n^{2}I_{n}-\frac{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}}{n}J_{n}\succeq 0
by the definition of
\widehat{\sigma}
. Define
\mathcal{U}_{s}=\{v:|v^{T}u_{2}|\geq s\}
, for
s\in[0,1]
. We parallel the utility analysis in Section 4.2 of Chaudhuri et al.
[
14
]
. We define
q_{F}(v)=\frac{\epsilon}{6D^{2}}v^{T}Mv
. The eigenvalues of
M
are
\sigma_{1}(M)\geq\cdots\geq\sigma_{n}(M)
. For
s\in[0,1]
, we then have (cf.
[
14
]
)

\displaystyle q_{F}(v)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\epsilon}{6D^{2}}\left(s^{2}\sigma_{1}(M)+(1-s^{2})\sigma_{n}(M)\right),

\displaystyle\text{for }v\in\mathcal{U}_{s},

\displaystyle q_{F}(v)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon}{6D^{2}}\left(s^{2}\sigma_{1}(M)+(1-s^{2})\sigma_{2}(M)\right),

\displaystyle\text{for }v\in\mathcal{U}^{c}_{s}.

Let
1-\rho^{2}=\frac{D^{2}}{\epsilon}
. For any
\tau\in[0,1]
(we choose this value later), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\hat{u}_{2}^{0}\in\mathcal{U}^{c}_{\rho})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{A,\widehat{\sigma}}[\mathbb{P}(\hat{u}_{2}^{0}\in\mathcal{U}^{c}_{\rho}|A,\widehat{\sigma})]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{A,\widehat{\sigma}}[\mathbb{P}(\hat{u}_{2}^{0}\in\mathcal{U}^{c}_{\rho}|A,\widehat{\sigma})\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}]+\mathbb{E}_{A,\widehat{\sigma}}[\mathbb{P}(\hat{u}_{2}^{0}\in\mathcal{U}^{c}_{\rho}|A,\widehat{\sigma})\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega^{c}}]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{A,\widehat{\sigma}}[\mathbb{P}(\hat{u}_{2}^{0}\in\mathcal{U}^{c}_{\rho}|A,\widehat{\sigma})\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}]+\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{A,\widehat{\sigma}}\left[\frac{\mathbb{P}(\hat{u}_{2}^{0}\in\mathcal{U}^{c}_{\rho}|A,\widehat{\sigma})}{\mathbb{P}(\hat{u}_{2}^{0}\in\mathcal{U}_{\tau}|A,\widehat{\sigma})}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}\right]+\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}.

(6)

Let
\mu
be the uniform measure on the unit sphere. By Lemma
33
, we have
\widehat{s}_{A^{2}}(v)=s_{A^{2}}(v)
on
\Omega
, for every
v
. We can then write

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{A,\widehat{\sigma}}\left[\frac{\mathbb{P}(\hat{u}_{2}^{0}\in\mathcal{U}_{\rho}^{c}|A,\widehat{\sigma})}{\mathbb{P}(\hat{u}_{2}^{0}\in\mathcal{U_{\tau}}|A,\widehat{\sigma})}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{A,\widehat{\sigma}}\left[\frac{\int_{\mathcal{U}^{c}_{\rho}}\mathrm{exp}(\frac{\epsilon}{6D^{2}}\widehat{s}^{\prime}_{A^{2}}(v))d\mu}{\int_{\mathcal{U}_{\tau}}\mathrm{exp}(\frac{\epsilon}{6D^{2}}\widehat{s}^{\prime}_{A^{2}}(v))d\mu}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{A,\widehat{\sigma}}\left[\frac{\int_{\mathcal{U}^{c}_{\rho}}\mathrm{exp}(\frac{\epsilon}{6D^{2}}(\widehat{s}_{A^{2}}(v)-\frac{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}}{n}v^{T}J_{n}v))d\mu}{\int_{\mathcal{U}_{\tau}}\mathrm{exp}(\frac{\epsilon}{6D^{2}}(\widehat{s}_{A^{2}}(v)-\frac{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}}{n}v^{T}J_{n}v))d\mu}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{A,\widehat{\sigma}}\left[\frac{\int_{\mathcal{U}^{c}_{\rho}}\mathrm{exp}(\frac{\epsilon}{6D^{2}}(s_{A^{2}}(v)-\frac{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}}{n}v^{T}J_{n}v))d\mu}{\int_{\mathcal{U}_{\tau}}\mathrm{exp}(\frac{\epsilon}{6D^{2}}(s_{A^{2}}(v)-\frac{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}}{n}v^{T}J_{n}v))d\mu}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{A,\widehat{\sigma}}\left[\frac{\int_{\mathcal{U}^{c}_{\rho}}\mathrm{exp}(\frac{\epsilon}{6D^{2}}(v^{T}A^{2}v-\frac{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}}{n}v^{T}J_{n}v))d\mu}{\int_{\mathcal{U}_{\tau}}\mathrm{exp}(\frac{\epsilon}{6D^{2}}(v^{T}A^{2}v-\frac{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}}{n}v^{T}J_{n}v))d\mu}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{A,\widehat{\sigma}}\left[\frac{\int_{\mathcal{U}^{c}_{\rho}}\mathrm{exp}(\frac{\epsilon}{6D^{2}}(v^{T}A^{2}v-\frac{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}}{n}v^{T}J_{n}v+n^{2}))d\mu}{\int_{\mathcal{U}_{\tau}}\mathrm{exp}(\frac{\epsilon}{6D^{2}}(v^{T}A^{2}v-\frac{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}}{n}v^{T}J_{n}v+n^{2}))d\mu}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{A,\widehat{\sigma}}\left[\frac{\int_{\mathcal{U}^{c}_{\rho}}\mathrm{exp}(\frac{\epsilon}{6D^{2}}(v^{T}A^{2}v-\frac{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}}{n}v^{T}J_{n}v+n^{2}||v||_{2}^{2}))d\mu}{\int_{\mathcal{U}_{\tau}}\mathrm{exp}(\frac{\epsilon}{6D^{2}}(v^{T}A^{2}v-\frac{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}}{n}v^{T}J_{n}v+n^{2}||v||_{2}^{2}))d\mu}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{A,\widehat{\sigma}}\left[\frac{\int_{\mathcal{U}^{c}_{\rho}}\mathrm{exp}(\frac{\epsilon}{6D^{2}}v^{T}Mv)d\mu}{\int_{\mathcal{U}_{\tau}}\mathrm{exp}(\frac{\epsilon}{6D^{2}}v^{T}Mv)d\mu}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}\right],

where we used the independence of
(A,\widehat{\sigma})
from
\mu
. We also used the fact that, under
\mu
, the input
v
satisfies
||v||_{2}=1
, and adding an extra
n^{2}
term does not change the ratio of the two densities. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{A,\widehat{\sigma}}\left[\frac{\mathbb{P}(\hat{u}_{2}^{0}\in\mathcal{U}^{c}_{\rho}|A,\widehat{\sigma})}{\mathbb{P}(\hat{u}_{2}^{0}\in\mathcal{U}_{\tau}|A,\widehat{\sigma})}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{A,\widehat{\sigma}}\left[\frac{\exp\left(\frac{\epsilon}{6D^{2}}\left(\rho^{2}\sigma_{1}(M)+(1-\rho^{2})\sigma_{2}(M)\right)\right)\mu(\mathcal{U}^{c}_{\rho})}{\exp\left(\frac{\epsilon}{6D^{2}}\left(\tau^{2}\sigma_{1}(M)+(1-\tau^{2})\sigma_{n}(M)\right)\right)\mu(\mathcal{U}_{\tau})}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{A,\widehat{\sigma}}\Bigg[\exp\left(\frac{-\epsilon}{6D^{2}}\left(\sigma^{2}\sigma_{1}(M)-\rho^{2}\sigma_{1}(M)-(1-\rho^{2})\sigma_{2}(M)\right)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\cdot\exp\left(\frac{n-1}{2}\log\frac{2}{1-\tau}\right)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}\Bigg],

where
\rho<\tau<1
, to make the upper bound less than
1
. We also used Lemma
6
in
[
14
]
. For the choice
1-\tau^{2}=\frac{1-\rho^{2}}{2}\left(1-\frac{\sigma_{2}(M)}{\sigma_{1}(M)}\right)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{A,\widehat{\sigma}}\left[\frac{\mathbb{P}(\hat{u}_{2}^{0}\in\mathcal{U}^{c}_{\rho}|A,\widehat{\sigma})}{\mathbb{P}(\hat{u}_{2}^{0}\in\mathcal{U}_{\tau}|A,\widehat{\sigma})}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{A,\widehat{\sigma}}\Bigg[\exp\Bigg(\frac{-\epsilon}{12D^{2}}\cdot(1-\rho^{2})(\sigma_{1}(M)-\sigma_{2}(M))+\frac{n-1}{2}\log\left(\frac{8\sigma_{1}(M)}{(1-\rho^{2})(\sigma_{1}(M)-\sigma_{2}(M))}\right)\Bigg)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}\Bigg].

Now we analyze the eigenvalues/singular values of
M
. By Lemma
33
, we have on
\Omega
that

\displaystyle||A^{2}-P^{2}||_{2}\leq(||A||_{2}+||P||_{2})||A-P||_{2}\leq(||A-P||_{2}+2\sigma_{1}(P))||A-P||_{2}\precsim d\sqrt{d}.

By Lemma
5
, we have

\displaystyle\sigma_{2}(M)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{2}\left(A^{2}+n^{2}I_{n}-\frac{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}}{n}J_{n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sigma_{2}\left(P^{2}-\frac{\sigma^{2}_{1}(P)}{n}J_{n}\right)+n^{2}+||A^{2}-P^{2}||_{2}+|\widehat{\sigma}^{2}-\sigma^{2}_{1}(P)|.

Also,

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}^{2}(A)+n^{2}+\widehat{\sigma}^{2}\geq\sigma_{1}\left(M\right)\geq\sigma_{1}\left(P^{2}-\frac{\sigma^{2}_{1}(P)}{n}J_{n}\right)+n^{2}-||A^{2}-P^{2}||_{2}-|\widehat{\sigma}^{2}-\sigma^{2}_{1}(P)|.

But since
\mathbf{1}_{n}
is the top eigenvector of
P
, the eigenvalues/singular values of
P^{2}-\frac{\sigma^{2}_{1}(P)}{n}J_{n}
are
\sigma^{2}_{2}(P)
, and
n-1
zeroes. Hence, on
\Omega
, we have

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}\left(M\right)-\sigma_{2}\left(M\right)

\displaystyle\geq\sigma_{2}^{2}(P)-2||A^{2}-P^{2}||_{2}-2|\widehat{\sigma}^{2}-\sigma^{2}_{1}(P)|\succsim d^{2}-d\sqrt{d}\succsim d^{2},

and

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}(M)=\sigma_{1}\left(A^{2}+n^{2}I_{n}-\frac{\widehat{\sigma}^{2}}{n}J_{n}\right)\precsim d^{2}+n^{2}+d^{2}\precsim n^{2}.

Hence,
\sigma_{1}(M)\leq n^{2}\log(n)
and
\sigma_{1}(M)-\sigma_{2}(M)\geq\frac{d^{2}}{\log(n)}
, for
n
large enough, on
\Omega
, by assumption. Thus, since
d>\sqrt{n}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{A,\widehat{\sigma}}\left[\frac{\mathbb{P}(\hat{u}_{2}^{0}\in\mathcal{U}^{c}_{\rho}|A,\widehat{\sigma})}{\mathbb{P}(\hat{u}_{2}^{0}\in\mathcal{U}_{\tau}|A,\widehat{\sigma})}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\mathbb{E}_{A,\widehat{\sigma}}\left[\exp\left(\frac{-\epsilon}{12\log(n)D^{2}}\cdot(1-\rho^{2})d^{2}+\frac{n-1}{2}\log\left(\frac{8n\log^{2}(n)}{1-\rho^{2}}\right)\right)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}\right].

Next, since
\epsilon<nD^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{8n\log^{2}(n)}{1-\rho^{2}}\right)=\log\left(\frac{8n\log^{2}(n)\epsilon}{D^{2}}\right)<\log(8\log^{2}(n)n^{2})<\frac{\log^{3}(n)}{12}=\frac{\epsilon\log^{3}(n)(1-\rho^{2})}{12D^{2}}.

Since
d^{2}>n\log^{4}(n)
, we obtain

\displaystyle(n-1)\log\left(\frac{8n\log^{2}(n)}{1-\rho^{2}}\right)<\frac{\epsilon d^{2}(1-\rho^{2})}{12\log(n)D^{2}},

so since
1-\rho^{2}<\frac{1}{\log(n)}
, we have
\log(n)\leq\frac{n-1}{2}\log\left(\frac{8n\log^{2}(n)}{1-\rho^{2}}\right)
, implying that

\displaystyle\log(n)+\frac{n-1}{2}\log\left(\frac{8n\log^{2}(n)}{1-\rho^{2}}\right)<\frac{\epsilon d^{2}(1-\rho^{2})}{12\log(n)D^{2}}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{A,\widehat{\sigma}}\left[\frac{\mathbb{P}(\hat{u}_{2}^{0}\in\mathcal{U}^{c}_{\rho}|A,\widehat{\sigma})}{\mathbb{P}(\hat{u}_{2}^{0}\in\mathcal{U}_{\tau}|A,\widehat{\sigma})}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega}\right]\leq\frac{1}{n}.

Using inequality (
C.5.2
), we obtain
\mathbb{P}(|u_{2}^{T}\hat{u}_{2}^{0}|^{2}>\rho^{2})\geq 1-\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}
, as required.

C.5.3
Proof of Lemma
35

Note that

\|A_{0}-A_{1}\|_{2}=\left\|(\widehat{\sigma}_{1}^{2}-\sigma_{1}^{2})\frac{\textbf{1}_{n}\textbf{1}_{n}^{T}}{n}\right\|_{2}=|\widehat{\sigma}_{1}^{2}-\sigma_{1}^{2}|.

Combined with the Davis-Kahan Theorem (cf. Lemma
9
), we then have

\displaystyle\mathop{\inf}_{q:q^{2}=1}||qu_{2}-u_{2}^{*}||_{2}=\mathop{\inf}_{q:q^{2}=1}||qu_{2}-u_{2}^{*}||_{F}\precsim\frac{|\widehat{\sigma}_{1}^{2}-\sigma_{1}^{2}|}{\lambda_{1}(A_{1})-\lambda_{2}(A_{1})}.

This completes the proof.

C.5.4
Proof of Lemma
36

Note that, since
\sigma_{1}^{2}
is the top eigenvalue of
P
, with associated eigenvector
\frac{\mathbf{1}_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}
, we have
\lambda_{1}(P_{1})=\sigma_{1}(P_{1})=\sigma_{2}^{2}(P)
. Since the rank of
P
is
2
, we have
\lambda_{2}(P_{1})=\sigma_{2}(P_{1})=\sigma_{3}^{2}(P)=0
. Hence, by Lemma
9
, we have

\displaystyle\mathop{\inf}_{q:q^{2}=1}||qu_{2}^{*}-u_{2,P}||_{2}=\mathop{\inf}_{q:q^{2}=1}||qu_{2}^{*}-u_{2,P}||_{F}\precsim\frac{||A^{2}-P^{2}||_{2}}{\sigma_{2}^{2}(P)},

completing the proof.

Appendix D
Proofs for Section
5

D.1
Proofs for Section
5.1

We first show that
\hat{L}_{\epsilon,\delta}(G)
is pure node DP and upper-bounds
LS_{T_{D}}(G)=\mathop{\max}\limits_{G^{\prime}\sim_{node}G}d_{node}(T_{D}(G),T_{D}(G^{\prime}))
, with high probability. We will use the following result:

Lemma 37

(Claim
17
in
[
6
]
)
.

The maps
T_{D}:\mathcal{G}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}_{n,2D}
and
d_{T_{D}}:\mathcal{G}_{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
obtained from solving the linear program (
3
) satisfy:

1.

d_{T_{D}}(G)=0
, for every
G\in\mathcal{G}_{n,D}
,

2.

d_{T_{D}}(G)\geq d_{node}(G,T_{D}(G))
, for every
G\in\mathcal{G}_{n}
,

3.

|d_{T_{D}}(G)-d_{T_{D}}(G^{\prime})|\leq 4
, for every
G^{\prime}\sim_{node}G
, and
G^{\prime},G\in\mathcal{G}_{n}
.

Moreover,
T_{D}(G)=G
, for
G\in\mathcal{G}_{n,D}
.

Lemma 38
.

Let
\delta\in(0,1)
, and let
T_{D}
,
d_{T_{D}}
, and
\hat{L}_{\epsilon,\delta}
be as in Definition
11
. Then
\hat{L}_{\epsilon,\delta}
is
(\epsilon,0)
-node DP and

\mathbb{P}(\hat{L}_{\epsilon,\delta}(G)\geq LS_{T_{D}}(G))\geq 1-\delta,\qquad\text{for every }G\in\mathcal{G}_{n}.

Proof.

Note that since
|d_{T_{D}}(G)-d_{T_{D}}(G^{\prime})|\leq 4
by Lemma
37
, for every
G^{\prime}\sim_{node}G
, and
G^{\prime},G\in\mathcal{G}_{n}
, the global sensitivity of
d_{T_{D}}
is bounded above by
4
. Hence, applying Laplace noise with parameter
\frac{8}{\epsilon}
guarantees
(\epsilon,0)
-node DP, and with post-processing,
\hat{L}_{\epsilon,\delta}
clearly satisfies
(\epsilon,0)
-node DP.

Next, we prove a deterministic upper bound on
LS_{T_{D}}(G)=\mathop{\max}\limits_{G^{\prime}\sim_{node}G}d_{node}(T_{D}(G),T_{D}(G^{\prime}))
. By the triangle inequality, for every
G^{\prime}\sim_{node}G
, we have

\displaystyle d_{node}(T_{D}(G),T_{D}(G^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\leq d_{node}(G,T_{D}(G))+d_{node}(G,G^{\prime})+d_{node}(G^{\prime},T_{D}(G^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\leq d_{node}(G,T_{D}(G))+1+d_{node}(G^{\prime},T_{D}(G^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\leq d_{T_{D}}(G)+1+d_{T_{D}}(G^{\prime})\leq 5+2d_{T_{D}}(G),

where we used the fact that
d_{T_{D}}(G)\geq d_{node}(G,T_{D}(G))
and
|d_{T_{D}}(G)-d_{T_{D}}(G^{\prime})|\leq 4
, for every
G^{\prime}\sim_{node}G
. Thus, we have
LS_{T_{D}}(G)\leq 5+2d_{T_{D}}(G)
for every
G\in\mathcal{G}_{n}
. In particular, this implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\hat{L}_{\epsilon,\delta}(G)<LS_{T_{D}}(G))

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(5+2d_{T_{D}}(G)+\mathrm{Lap}\left(\frac{8}{\epsilon}\right)+\frac{8\log(1/\delta)}{\epsilon}<LS_{T_{D}}(G)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\mathrm{Lap}\left(\frac{8}{\epsilon}\right)<-\frac{8\log(1/\delta)}{\epsilon}\right)\leq\delta,

as required.
∎

D.1.1
Proof of Theorem
2

Fix
G\sim_{node}G^{\prime}
, with
G,G^{\prime}\in\mathcal{G}_{n}
. To simplify notation, let
\mathcal{A}^{(G)}:=\mathcal{A}^{(\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}(G),\delta_{2}^{\prime}(G))}
. Define

\displaystyle A_{G}

\displaystyle:=(\hat{L}_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(G),\mathcal{A}^{(G)}(T_{D}(G))),

\displaystyle A_{G^{\prime}}

\displaystyle:=(\hat{L}_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(G^{\prime}),\mathcal{A}^{(G^{\prime})}(T_{D}(G^{\prime}))),

\displaystyle A_{m}

\displaystyle:=(\hat{L}_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(G),\mathcal{A}^{(G)}(T_{D}(G^{\prime}))).

Let
P_{G}
,
P_{G^{\prime}}
, and
P_{m}
be the distributions of
A_{G}
,
A_{G^{\prime}}
, and
A_{m}
, respectively.
Let
E
be an arbitrary measurable set in the image of
(\hat{L}_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(\cdot),\mathcal{A}^{(\cdot)}(T_{D}(\cdot)))
. By the privacy of
\hat{L}_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}
we have

\displaystyle P_{G^{\prime}}(E)=\mathbb{P}\left((\hat{L}_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(G^{\prime}),\mathcal{A}^{(G^{\prime})}(T_{D}(G^{\prime})))\in E\right)\leq e^{\epsilon_{1}}P_{m}(E).

Let
F=\left\{\hat{L}_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(G)\geq LS_{{T_{D}}}(G)\right\}
. We have from Lemma
38
that
\mathbb{P}(F^{c})\leq\delta_{1}
. Let
y_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}
be the pdf of
Y_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(G)=5+2d_{T_{D}}(G)+\mathrm{Lap}\left(\frac{8}{\epsilon_{1}}\right)+\frac{8\log(1/\delta_{1})}{\epsilon_{1}}
, so
\hat{L}_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(G)=\max\left\{\frac{1}{2},Y_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(G)\right\}
. If
LS_{T_{D}}(G)=0
, then
T_{D}(G)=T_{D}(G^{\prime})
, and

P_{m}(E|F)=P_{G}(E|F)\leq e^{2\epsilon_{2}}P_{G}(E|F)+\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}.

Now assume
LS_{T_{D}}(G)\geq 1
. Then

\displaystyle P_{m}(E|F)=\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{(\hat{L}_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(G),\mathcal{A}^{(G)}(T_{D}(G^{\prime})))\in E\right\}\bigcap F\right)}{\mathbb{P}(F)}

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{LS_{{T_{D}}}(G)}^{\infty}\frac{\mathbb{P}\left((\max\{1/2,x\},\mathcal{A}^{(\epsilon_{2}/\max\{1/2,x\},\delta_{2}/\max\{1/2,x\})}(T_{D}(G^{\prime})))\in E|Y_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(G)=x\right)y_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(x)}{\mathbb{P}(F)}dx

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{LS_{{T_{D}}}(G)}^{\infty}\frac{\mathbb{P}\left((x,\mathcal{A}^{(\epsilon_{2}/x,\delta_{2}/x)}(T_{D}(G^{\prime})))\in E\right)y_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(x)}{\mathbb{P}(F)}dx,

where we dropped the conditioning because
Y_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(G)
is independent of
\mathcal{A}^{(\epsilon_{2}/x,\delta_{2}/x)}(T_{D}(G^{\prime}))
. Also, since
\max\left\{\frac{1}{2},x\right\}\geq LS_{T_{D}}(G)\geq 1
, we must have
x\geq LS_{T_{D}}(G)\geq 1
. Now fix
x\geq LS_{{T_{D}}}(G)
. Since
2\epsilon_{2}<\log(1/(\delta_{2}\cdot\mathrm{poly}(n)))
and
x\geq LS_{T_{D}}(G)\geq 1
, we have
\frac{2\epsilon_{2}}{x}<\log\left(\frac{x}{\delta_{2}}\right)
. Since
\mathcal{A}^{(\epsilon_{2}/x,\delta_{2}/x)}
is
\left(\frac{2\epsilon_{2}}{x},\frac{\delta_{2}}{x}\right)_{2D}
-node DP, it is clearly
\left(\frac{2\epsilon_{2}}{LS_{T_{D}}(G)},\frac{\delta_{2}}{LS_{T_{D}}(G)}\right)_{2D}
-node DP. By group privacy of size
LS_{T_{D}}(G)
, we obtain
\left(2\epsilon_{2},\delta_{2}e^{2\epsilon_{2}}\right)_{2D}
-node DP on groups of size
LS_{T_{D}}(G)
. Since
\delta_{2}<e^{-2\epsilon_{2}}/\mathrm{poly}(n)
, this is
(2\epsilon_{2},1/\mathrm{poly}(n))_{2D}
-node DP. Now, when changing
G^{\prime}
to
G
, we change at most
LS_{T_{D}}(G)
nodes to go from
T_{D}(G^{\prime})
to
T_{D}(G)
. Hence, we have

\displaystyle P_{m}(E|F)\leq\int_{LS_{{T_{D}}}(G)}^{\infty}\frac{\left(e^{2\epsilon_{2}}\mathbb{P}\left((x,\mathcal{A}^{(\epsilon_{2}/x,\delta_{2}/x)}(T_{D}(G)))\in E\right)+1/\mathrm{poly}(n)\right)y_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(x)}{\mathbb{P}(F)}dx

\displaystyle\quad=e^{2\epsilon_{2}}\int_{LS_{{T_{D}}}(G)}^{\infty}\frac{\mathbb{P}\left((x,\mathcal{A}^{(\epsilon_{2}/x,\delta_{2}/x)}(T_{D}(G)))\in E\right)y_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(x)}{\mathbb{P}(F)}dx+\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}

\displaystyle\quad=e^{2\epsilon_{2}}\int_{LS_{{T_{D}}}(G)}^{\infty}\frac{\mathbb{P}\left((\max\{1/2,x\},\mathcal{A}^{(\epsilon_{2}/\max\{1/2,x\},\delta_{2}/\max\{1/2,x\})}(T_{D}(G)))\in E|Y_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(G)=x\right)y_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(x)}{\mathbb{P}(F)}dx

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{e^{2\epsilon_{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{(\hat{L}_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(G),\mathcal{A}^{(G)}(T_{D}(G)))\in E\right\}\cap F\right)}{\mathbb{P}(F)}+\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}

\displaystyle\quad=e^{2\epsilon_{2}}P_{G}(E|F)+\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}.

Note now that
P_{G}(F)=P_{m}(F)
, since in both cases, we have
\hat{L}_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(G)
, not
\hat{L}_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(G^{\prime})
. Thus, we have

\displaystyle P_{m}(E\cap F)\leq e^{2\epsilon_{2}}P_{G}(E\cap F)+\frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(n)}.

Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle P_{G^{\prime}}(E)

\displaystyle\leq e^{\epsilon_{1}}(P_{m}(E\cap F)+\delta_{1})\leq e^{\epsilon_{1}}(e^{2\epsilon_{2}}P_{G}(E\cap F)+1/\mathrm{poly}(n))+e^{\epsilon_{1}}\delta_{1}

\displaystyle\leq e^{\epsilon_{1}+2\epsilon_{2}}P_{G}(E)+e^{\epsilon_{1}}(1/\mathrm{poly}(n)+\delta_{1}).

Thus,
(\hat{L}_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(G),\mathcal{A}^{(G)}(T_{D}(G)))
is
(\epsilon_{1}+2\epsilon_{2},e^{\epsilon_{1}}(1/\mathrm{poly}(n)+\delta_{1}))
-node DP, as required.

D.1.2
Proof of Theorem
3

Using the same notation and steps as in the proof of Theorem
2
, we have by the privacy of
\hat{L}_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}
that

\displaystyle P_{G^{\prime}}(E)=\mathbb{P}\left((\hat{L}_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(G^{\prime}),\mathcal{A}^{\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}(G^{\prime})}(T_{D}(G^{\prime})))\in E\right)\leq e^{\epsilon_{1}}P_{m}(E).

Let
F=\left\{\hat{L}_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(G)\geq LS_{{T_{D}}}(G)\right\}
. Let
y_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}
be the pdf of

Y_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(G)=5+2d_{T_{D}}(G)+\mathrm{Lap}\left(\frac{8}{\epsilon_{1}}\right)+\frac{8\log(1/\delta_{1})}{\epsilon_{1}}.

If
LS_{T_{D}}(G)=0
, then
T_{D}(G)=T_{D}(G^{\prime})
and
P_{m}(E|F)=P_{G}(E|F)\leq e^{2\epsilon_{2}}P_{G}(E|F)
. Now assume
LS_{T_{D}}(G)\geq 1
. We have

\displaystyle P_{m}(E|F)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{(\hat{L}_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(G),\mathcal{A}^{\left(\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}(G)\right)}(T_{D}(G^{\prime})))\in E\right\}\cap F\right)}{\mathbb{P}(F)}

\displaystyle=\int_{LS_{T_{D}}(G)}^{\infty}\frac{\mathbb{P}\left((\max\{1/2,x\},\mathcal{A}^{(\epsilon_{2}/\max\{1/2,x\})}(T_{D}(G^{\prime})))\in E|Y_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(G)=x\right)y_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(x)}{\mathbb{P}(F)}dx

\displaystyle=\int_{LS_{T_{D}}(G)}^{\infty}\frac{\mathbb{P}\left((x,\mathcal{A}^{(\epsilon_{2}/x)}(T_{D}(G^{\prime})))\in E\right)y_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(x)}{\mathbb{P}(F)}dx,

where we dropped the conditioning because
Y_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(G)
is independent of
\mathcal{A}^{(\epsilon_{2}/x)}(T_{D}(G^{\prime}))
. Also, since
\max\left\{\frac{1}{2},x\right\}\geq LS_{T_{D}}(G)\geq 1
, we must have
x\geq LS_{T_{D}}(G)\geq 1
. Now,
\mathcal{A}^{(\epsilon_{2}/x)}(T_{D}(G^{\prime}))
is
\left(\frac{\epsilon_{2}}{x},0\right)_{2D}
-node DP. Hence, since
x\geq LS_{T_{D}}(G)
, we know that
\mathcal{A}^{(\epsilon_{2}/x)}(T_{D}(G^{\prime}))
is
\left(\frac{\epsilon_{2}}{LS_{T_{D}}(G)},0\right)_{2D}
-node DP. Next, when changing
G^{\prime}
to
G
, we change at most
LS_{T_{D}}(G)
nodes to go from
T_{D}(G^{\prime})
to
T_{D}(G)
. Given that
\mathcal{A}^{(\epsilon_{2}/x)}
is
\left(\frac{\epsilon_{2}}{LS_{T_{D}}(G)},0\right)_{2D}
-node DP, the privacy parameter changes to
\frac{LS_{T_{D}}(G)\epsilon_{2}}{LS_{T_{D}}(G)}=\epsilon_{2}
, using group privacy. Hence, we have

\displaystyle P_{m}(E|F)\leq e^{\epsilon_{2}}\int_{LS_{T_{D}}(G)}^{\infty}\frac{\mathbb{P}\left((x,\mathcal{A}^{(\epsilon_{2}/x)}(T_{D}(G)))\in E\right)y_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(x)}{\mathbb{P}(F)}dx

\displaystyle\quad=e^{\epsilon_{2}}\int_{LS_{T_{D}}(G)}^{\infty}\frac{\mathbb{P}\left((\max\{1/2,x\},\mathcal{A}^{(\epsilon_{2}/\max\{1/2,x\})}(T_{D}(G)))\in E|Y_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(G)=x\right)y_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(x)}{\mathbb{P}(F)}dx

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{e^{\epsilon_{2}}\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{(\hat{L}_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(G),\mathcal{A}^{\left(\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}(G)\right)}(T_{D}(G)))\in E\right\}\cap F\right)}{\mathbb{P}(F)}=e^{\epsilon_{2}}P_{G}(E|F).

Now note that
P_{G}(F)=P_{m}(F)
, since in both cases, we have
\hat{L}_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(G)
, not
\hat{L}_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(G^{\prime})
. Thus, we have

\displaystyle P_{m}(E\cap F)\leq e^{\epsilon_{2}}P_{G}(E\cap F).

Hence, using Lemma
38
, we obtain

\displaystyle P_{G^{\prime}}(E)\leq e^{\epsilon_{1}}(P_{m}(E\cap F)+\delta_{1})\leq e^{\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}}P_{G}(E\cap F)+e^{\epsilon_{1}}\delta_{1}\leq e^{\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}}P_{G}(E)+e^{\epsilon_{1}}\delta_{1}.

Thus,
(\hat{L}_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}}(G),\mathcal{A}^{\left(\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}(G)\right)}(T_{D}(G)))
is
(\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2},e^{\epsilon_{1}}\delta_{1})
-node DP, as required.

D.1.3
Proof of Proposition
1

By basic composition (cf. Lemma
18
), if each
\widetilde{\theta}(G_{j})
satisfies
(\epsilon,\delta)
-node DP, the algorithm
\widehat{\theta}(G)
satisfies
(T\epsilon,T\delta)
-node DP. Importantly, we also have the inequality

\mathbb{P}\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}(\widehat{\theta}(G),\theta)\leq\xi T\right)\geq\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{T}1\left\{\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}(\widetilde{\theta}(G_{j}),\theta)\leq\xi\right\}\geq\frac{T+1}{2}\right).

(7)

Indeed, suppose at least
\frac{T+1}{2}
subgraph assignments have misclassification rate bounded by
\xi
. WLOG, reindex the subgraphs so that the estimates
\widetilde{\theta}(G_{1}),\dots,\widetilde{\theta}(G_{\ell})
are all within
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
-distance
\xi
of
\theta
, where
\ell=\frac{T+1}{2}
. We claim that this implies the aggregated estimator
\widehat{\theta}(G)
has misclassification rate bounded by
\xi T
. Note that the boosting algorithm will certainly output an index
j^{*}
satisfying the criterion, since any of the
\{G_{j}\}_{j=1}^{\ell}
are within
\mathcal{L}
-distance
2\xi
of all the rest, since
\mathcal{L}\leq\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
and
\mathcal{L}
satisfies the triangle inequality. Furthermore, there must exist
\ell^{*}\in[\ell]
such that
\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\theta}(G_{j^{*}}),\widetilde{\theta}(G_{\ell^{*}}))\leq 2\xi
. In addition, since
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}(\widetilde{\theta}(G_{\ell^{*}}),\theta)\leq\xi
by assumption, we have
\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\theta}(G_{j^{*}}),\theta)\leq 3\xi
. We now prove the following lemma:

Lemma 39
.

Suppose
\theta
has all communities of size
m=\frac{n}{k}
. Let
\widehat{\theta}_{1},\widehat{\theta}_{2}\in[k]^{n}
, and define
\Theta,\Theta_{1},\Theta_{2}\in\mathbf{M}_{n\times k}
accordingly. Suppose
\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\theta}_{1},\theta)\leq\eta
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}(\widehat{\theta}_{2},\theta)\leq\xi
. Let
J_{i}\in\arg\min_{J\in E_{k}}\|\widehat{\Theta}_{i}J-\Theta\|_{0}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
. If
\frac{\xi+2\eta k+\xi k}{2}<1
, then the unique minimizer of
\min_{J\in E_{k}}\|\Theta_{2}J-\Theta_{1}\|_{0}
is given by
J=J_{2}J_{1}^{-1}
.

Proof.

By assumption, after applying
J_{1}
, the community assignment
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
has at most
\frac{\eta n}{2}
misclassified nodes, whereas
\widehat{\theta}_{2}
has at most
\frac{\xi m}{2}
misclassified nodes in each true community. Thus, under the permutation
J_{2}J_{1}^{-1}
, the disagreement between
\widehat{\theta}_{1}
and
\widehat{\theta}_{2}
is at most
\frac{(\eta+\xi)n}{2}
. Now consider any other permutation, which must misclassify at least one community relative to
\Theta_{1}
. The number of misclassified nodes in that community must be at least

m-\frac{\xi m}{2}-\frac{\eta n}{2}=m\left(1-\frac{\xi}{2}-\frac{\eta k}{2}\right).

We can check that

m\left(1-\frac{\xi}{2}-\frac{\eta k}{2}\right)>\frac{(\eta+\xi)n}{2}

under the stated condition, concluding the proof.
∎

Note that
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}(\widetilde{\theta}(G_{j}),\theta)\leq\xi
for all
j\in[\ell]
by assumption and
\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\theta}(G_{j}^{*}),\theta)\leq 3\xi
. Applying Lemma
39
with
\eta=3\xi
